We provide a rigorous convergence analysis for HALoS under non-convex objectives, including theoretical guarantees on the role of hierarchical momentum in asynchronous training. Empirically, HALoS attains up to 7.5x faster convergence than synchronous baselines in geo-distributed LLM training and improves upon existing asynchronous methods by up to 2.1x. Crucially, HALoS preserves the model quality of fully synchronous SGD-matching or exceeding accuracy on standard language modeling and downstream benchmarks-while substantially lowering total training time. These results demonstrate that hierarchical, server-side update accumulation and global model merging are powerful tools for scalable, efficient training of new-era LLMs in heterogeneous, geo-distributed environments.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces HALOS, a hierarchical asynchronous local SGD framework designed for training large language models (LLMs) across geographically distributed and heterogeneous hardware.  HALOS utilizes local parameter servers (LPSs) within each region to asynchronously aggregate gradients and a global parameter server (GPS) to merge updates across regions. This architecture aims to minimize expensive inter-region communication, mitigate the impact of stragglers, and leverage faster intra-region links.  The paper provides a convergence analysis for HALOS under non-convex objectives, explores the role of hierarchical momentum, and presents empirical results demonstrating faster convergence compared to synchronous and asynchronous baselines while maintaining or improving model quality.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The hierarchical asynchronous architecture with server-side update accumulation is a significant contribution. Prior work, such as DiLoCo and Async-Local-SGD, addresses communication efficiency, but HALOS appears to more effectively decouple computation from communication by introducing the intermediate LPS layer and the accompanying update accumulation.  The formal convergence analysis, specifically considering hierarchical momentum and delay in asynchronous training, adds further theoretical value.

*   **Significance:** LLM training is increasingly bottlenecked by communication costs in geo-distributed environments.  HALOS offers a practical solution to this problem, potentially democratizing access to LLM training resources and enabling more efficient utilization of existing infrastructure. The empirical results, demonstrating substantial speedups and preserved or improved model quality, strongly support the claims. A well-conducted and rigorously controlled experimental section is crucial.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   The hierarchical architecture is well-motivated and addresses the limitations of existing approaches.
    *   The convergence analysis provides theoretical justification for the approach.
    *   The empirical results are convincing and demonstrate significant performance gains.
    *   The ablation studies provide insights into the importance of different components of HALOS.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   While the paper mentions data heterogeneity, the analysis of it is shallow.  While IID data is assumed for the convergence proof, a more thorough analysis of non-IID scenarios, even if empirical, would further strengthen the paper's practical applicability.
    *  The work is limited to trace-driven simulations. A real-world distributed deployment with actual hardware and network conditions would substantially strengthen the claim.
    *   The algorithm's sensitivity to hyperparameter tuning.  While the paper performs an ablation study, the range of values explored for each parameter might not be exhaustive. A more robust hyperparameter optimization strategy could further improve performance.

*   **Potential Influence:** This paper could influence the development of more scalable and efficient LLM training frameworks.  The hierarchical asynchronous architecture and the insights on hierarchical momentum could be adopted by other researchers and practitioners.  The work also highlights the importance of considering both communication costs and hardware heterogeneity when designing distributed training systems.

*   **Justification for Score:** This is a good submission, but there are some limitations to the work that would hold it back from reaching the top percentiles. The paper introduces a relatively novel and practical method for LLM training in geo-distributed environments. The convergence analysis is appreciated, but is not significantly novel relative to prior work in distributed optimization. The work could be strengthened by better analysing the potential effect of hyperparameter selection, and conducting experiments in non-IID environments.

### hdl2v: A Code Translation Dataset for Enhanced LLM Verilog Generation
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.04544v1)
- **Authors**: Charles Hong, Brendan Roberts, Huijae An, Alex Um, Advay Ratan, Yakun Sophia Shao
- **Abstract**: Large language models (LLMs) are playing an increasingly large role in domains such as code generation, including hardware code generation, where Verilog is the key language. However, the amount of publicly available Verilog code pales in comparison to the amount of code available for software languages like Python. In this work, we present hdl2v ("HDL-to-Verilog"), a dataset which seeks to increase the amount of available human-written Verilog data by translating or compiling three other hardware description languages - VHDL, Chisel, and PyMTL3 - to Verilog. Furthermore, we demonstrate the value of hdl2v in enhancing LLM Verilog generation by improving performance of a 32 billion-parameter open-weight model by up to 23% (pass@10) in VerilogEvalV2, without utilizing any data augmentation or knowledge distillation from larger models. We also show hdl2v's ability to boost the performance of a data augmentation-based fine-tuning approach by 63%. Finally, we characterize and analyze our dataset to better understand which characteristics of HDL-to-Verilog datasets can be expanded upon in future work for even better performance.
- **Summary**: Okay, I've analyzed the provided OCR'd text and here's a summary, evaluation, and novelty assessment of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces hdl2v, a new dataset for enhancing Large Language Model (LLM) based Verilog code generation.  The dataset consists of 46,549 pairs of hardware description language (HDL) code (VHDL, Chisel, and PyMTL3) translated/compiled to Verilog. The authors demonstrate that fine-tuning an LLM (Qwen2.5-Coder-32B-Instruct) with hdl2v improves Verilog generation performance (measured by pass@1 and pass@10 on VerilogEvalV2) by up to 23% without data augmentation. They also demonstrate hdl2v's utility in boosting data augmentation approaches and analyze the characteristics of the dataset, including perplexity and token diversity. Ablation studies assess the importance of language choice (VHDL vs C) and code readability, concluding that learning primarily comes from the code, and syntax matters, with VHDL outperforming C.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Strengths:**
    * **Novel Dataset:** The primary strength is the creation and release of a new, sizable dataset for Verilog generation, specifically focusing on translations from other HDLs. Addressing the data scarcity problem for Verilog is a significant contribution. The authors have released the dataset for public usage, which will certainly help other researchers in the domain.
    * **Performance Improvement:**  The experiments clearly demonstrate the effectiveness of hdl2v in improving LLM Verilog generation performance, both independently and in conjunction with data augmentation techniques. The reported improvements on VerilogEvalV2 are significant and well-quantified.
    * **Ablation Studies:**  The ablation studies (C vs. VHDL and Readability analysis) provide valuable insights into what makes a dataset effective for LLM Verilog generation. This helps in understanding the limitations of the approach as well as suggesting future work.  The analysis is data driven and contributes to a deeper understanding of the underlying mechanisms.
    * **Clear and Well-Structured:** The paper is well-written, clearly structured, and presents the experiments and results in a thorough and easy-to-understand manner.

* **Weaknesses:**
    * **Limited Scope of Ablation Studies:** While the ablation studies are insightful, they could be more extensive. For example, a deeper dive into the characteristics of the designs themselves (e.g., size, complexity) would be useful. It's possible some design styles in VHDL are easier to translate than others, and that isn't addressed.
    * **Dependence on Machine Translation/Compilation:** The quality of the hdl2v dataset depends on the quality of the translation/compilation tools (vhd2vl and the Chisel/PyMTL3 compilers). Errors in these tools could introduce noise into the dataset. The authors could have included a brief investigation of the error rates of the tooling.
    * **Model Size impact:** A lot of the improvement metrics are tied to using only the base LLM (Qwen2.5-Coder-32B-Instruct). The scaling relationship is not studied if model size increases.
    * **Limited Baseline Comparison:** While BetterV [28] is considered as a baseline, there is no direct comparison with other Verilog generation specific datasets that use text augmentation or synthetic data generation.

* **Novelty:**

The paper's primary novelty lies in the *approach* of leveraging *existing* HDL code in other languages to augment Verilog datasets. While fine-tuning and data augmentation have been explored before, the idea of translating from other HDLs to specifically address the Verilog data scarcity problem is relatively new. Translating Verilog to C has also been done, but the observation in this paper is that using other HDLs produces better results. The paper is also the first to present datasets based on Chisel and PyMTL3 translation into Verilog.

* **Significance:**

The paper addresses a crucial bottleneck in applying LLMs to hardware design - the limited availability of Verilog data. By introducing hdl2v, the authors provide a valuable resource for researchers and practitioners in this field. The improvements in Verilog generation performance, even with a relatively modest fine-tuning approach, highlight the potential of this dataset. The insights from the ablation studies can guide future research on creating more effective datasets for code generation.

**Overall Assessment and Score:**

The hdl2v dataset is a useful contribution to the field of LLM-based hardware design. The authors address a key challenge (data scarcity) with a novel approach (HDL translation), and demonstrate its effectiveness through well-designed experiments. The ablation studies offer valuable insights. The weaknesses relate primarily to the depth of analysis and dependence on translation/compilation quality. Overall, I believe it is a worthwhile contribution to the community, deserving of a solid score.

- **Score**: 7/10

### Perceptual Decoupling for Scalable Multi-modal Reasoning via Reward-Optimized Captioning
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.04559v1)
- **Authors**: Yunhao Gou, Kai Chen, Zhili Liu, Lanqing Hong, Xin Jin, Zhenguo Li, James T. Kwok, Yu Zhang
- **Abstract**: Recent advances in slow-thinking language models (e.g., OpenAI-o1 and DeepSeek-R1) have demonstrated remarkable abilities in complex reasoning tasks by emulating human-like reflective cognition. However, extending such capabilities to multi-modal large language models (MLLMs) remains challenging due to the high cost of retraining vision-language alignments when upgrading the underlying reasoner LLMs. A straightforward solution is to decouple perception from reasoning, i.e., converting visual inputs into language representations (e.g., captions) that are then passed to a powerful text-only reasoner. However, this decoupling introduces a critical challenge: the visual extractor must generate descriptions that are both faithful to the image and informative enough to support accurate downstream reasoning. To address this, we propose Reasoning-Aligned Perceptual Decoupling via Caption Reward Optimization (RACRO) - a reasoning-guided reinforcement learning strategy that aligns the extractor's captioning behavior with the reasoning objective. By closing the perception-reasoning loop via reward-based optimization, RACRO significantly enhances visual grounding and extracts reasoning-optimized representations. Experiments on multi-modal math and science benchmarks show that the proposed RACRO method achieves state-of-the-art average performance while enabling superior scalability and plug-and-play adaptation to more advanced reasoning LLMs without the necessity for costly multi-modal re-alignment.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces RACRO (Reasoning-Aligned Perceptual Decoupling via Caption Reward Optimization), a framework designed to improve multi-modal reasoning in large language models (MLLMs).  RACRO addresses the challenge of aligning visual perception with reasoning capabilities, particularly when upgrading the underlying text-based LLMs. The core idea is to decouple the perception and reasoning modules: a vision extractor generates captions from images, and a separate, powerful text-only reasoner processes those captions to answer questions. To ensure the captions are both accurate and reasoning-relevant, the authors propose a reinforcement learning (RL) strategy (Caption Reward Optimization - CRO) that aligns the extractor's captioning behavior with the reasoning objective, by training a reward based on the correctness of the reasoner's output using the caption. Experiments on multi-modal math and science benchmarks demonstrate that RACRO achieves state-of-the-art average performance, while also offering superior scalability by allowing for the seamless integration of more advanced text-only LLMs, without needing to retrain the vision-language alignment.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's novelty lies primarily in the specific way it combines perceptual decoupling with a reward-optimized captioning strategy that enables seamless upgrade of underlying LLMs. Decoupling vision and language models is not entirely new, but the rigorous integration with reasoning outcomes as rewards and using that feedback to refine the captioning process adds a significant layer of innovation, that goes beyond standard image captioning tasks. The design and details for the CRO itself are quite important for the work.

*   **Significance:** The paper's significance stems from its practical solution to the scalability challenges of MLLMs. By avoiding the need for extensive and computationally costly multi-modal retraining when upgrading the underlying LLM, RACRO allows for a more modular and adaptable approach. This could accelerate the development and deployment of MLLMs, particularly in scenarios where access to massive multi-modal datasets and computational resources is limited. This provides a great solution for those in research.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Clear Problem Definition:** The paper clearly identifies and articulates the scalability problem in MLLMs.
    *   **Well-Defined Solution:** RACRO is presented as a coherent and well-engineered framework. The CRO component is well-motivated and effectively implemented.
    *   **Strong Experimental Results:** The paper provides ample empirical evidence to support its claims. The method consistently achieves state-of-the-art results across a range of benchmark datasets. The ablation studies are informative, providing insights into the contribution of each component.
    *   **Scalability Demonstrated:** RACRO reveals better scalability when compare to the traditional vision alignment, while remaining computationally efficient.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Reliance on Pre-trained Models:** The framework heavily relies on the quality of the pre-trained MLLM for visual extraction and the text-only LLM for reasoning. While the CRO mechanism mitigates some of the limitations of the initial visual extraction, it might not be able to completely overcome significant biases or limitations in these base models.
    *   **Reward Design:** The reward function is relatively simple, based on whether the reasoner produces the correct answer. More sophisticated reward functions that consider the quality of reasoning steps or the completeness of the captions could potentially lead to further improvements.
    *   **Computational Cost of CRO Training:** While RACRO avoids the cost of full MLLM retraining, the CRO stage itself still requires significant computational resources for RL training, involving caption rollouts and evaluating reasoning outcomes, which may be a barrier for some researchers.

*   **Potential Influence:** RACRO has the potential to influence future research in multi-modal reasoning by establishing a new approach to vision-language alignment that is scalable, modular, and adaptable. The idea of using reasoning outcomes as feedback to improve perceptual modules could be applied to a wider range of tasks and architectures. Furthermore, the plug-and-play compatibility of RACRO could encourage the development of more specialized and composable multi-modal systems.

**Justification for Score:**

I am assigning a score of **8** to this paper.

The novelty and significance of the work are clear, providing a practical and scalable approach to improving MLLM reasoning. The experimental results are strong and the paper is well-written. The framework tackles a challenging problem in an innovative way, offering a good solution that avoids the heavy computation needed by previous methods. While the paper has its weaknesses, the potential benefits for the multi-modal reasoning community are significant, demonstrating a better path forward for continual MLLM improvements as underlying LLMs progress.

### From Standalone LLMs to Integrated Intelligence: A Survey of Compound Al Systems
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.04565v1)
- **Authors**: Jiayi Chen, Junyi Ye, Guiling Wang
- **Abstract**: Compound Al Systems (CAIS) is an emerging paradigm that integrates large language models (LLMs) with external components, such as retrievers, agents, tools, and orchestrators, to overcome the limitations of standalone models in tasks requiring memory, reasoning, real-time grounding, and multimodal understanding. These systems enable more capable and context-aware behaviors by composing multiple specialized modules into cohesive workflows. Despite growing adoption in both academia and industry, the CAIS landscape remains fragmented, lacking a unified framework for analysis, taxonomy, and evaluation. In this survey, we define the concept of CAIS, propose a multi-dimensional taxonomy based on component roles and orchestration strategies, and analyze four foundational paradigms: Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG), LLM Agents, Multimodal LLMs (MLLMs), and orchestration-centric architectures. We review representative systems, compare design trade-offs, and summarize evaluation methodologies across these paradigms. Finally, we identify key challenges-including scalability, interoperability, benchmarking, and coordination-and outline promising directions for future research. This survey aims to provide researchers and practitioners with a comprehensive foundation for understanding, developing, and advancing the next generation of system-level artificial intelligence.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "From Standalone LLMs to Integrated Intelligence: A Survey of Compound AI Systems":

**Summary:**

This survey paper defines and analyzes Compound AI Systems (CAIS), which integrate Large Language Models (LLMs) with external components like retrievers, agents, tools, and orchestrators. The paper proposes a multi-dimensional taxonomy to categorize CAIS based on component roles and orchestration strategies. It then examines four foundational paradigms: Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG), LLM Agents, Multimodal LLMs (MLLMs), and Orchestration Frameworks. The survey reviews representative systems within each paradigm, compares design trade-offs, and summarizes evaluation methodologies. Finally, it identifies key challenges (scalability, interoperability, benchmarking, coordination) and outlines potential research directions.  The paper aims to offer a comprehensive foundation for understanding, developing, and advancing system-level artificial intelligence.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Comprehensive Scope:** The paper provides a broad and well-organized overview of a rapidly evolving field. It effectively synthesizes fragmented research into a coherent framework.
    *   **Clear Definitions and Taxonomy:** The paper's clear definition of CAIS and the proposed multi-dimensional taxonomy are valuable contributions.  They help to structure the field and facilitate comparisons between different systems.
    *   **Practical Relevance:** By highlighting design trade-offs, challenges, and evaluation methods, the paper offers practical guidance for researchers and practitioners developing CAIS.
    *   **Well-Structured:** The paper's organization is logical and easy to follow, making it accessible to a wide audience. The inclusion of figures further aids in understanding the complex concepts.
*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Limited Depth in Some Areas:** Due to the breadth of the topic, the paper cannot delve deeply into every aspect of each paradigm. Certain sections, especially those concerning the intricacies of multimodal integration and specific tool implementations, might benefit from more in-depth analysis.
    *   **Rapidly Evolving Field:** Given the speed at which LLMs and CAIS are developing, some of the specific systems and technologies discussed may become outdated relatively quickly. It inherently creates a short lifespan.
    *   **Evaluation Methodology:** While the paper summarizes evaluation methods, a more critical analysis of the strengths and weaknesses of different benchmarks and metrics would be valuable. The discussion of evaluating ethical dimensions is also limited.

*   **Novelty and Significance:**

    *   **Novelty:** The primary novelty lies in the paper's effort to provide a unified framework and taxonomy for analyzing Compound AI Systems. While surveys on individual components (RAG, LLM Agents) exist, this is among the first attempts to provide a holistic, system-level perspective.
    *   **Significance:** This survey is significant because it addresses a critical need for understanding the move from standalone LLMs to more complex, integrated systems. The field's increasing complexity necessitates a structured framework like the one proposed in this paper. This paper is also timely, as the trend toward CAIS is rapidly increasing and the need for understanding CAIS in terms of its limitations and scalability constraints is becoming critical.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence the field by:

    *   Providing a shared vocabulary and conceptual framework for researchers.
    *   Guiding the design and development of future CAIS.
    *   Highlighting important research directions and open challenges.
    *   Improving the evaluation and benchmarking of CAIS.
    * It can aid in better organizing future research and making it easier for researchers to understand different aspects of CAIS.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper is a well-written and timely survey that addresses a critical need for organization and analysis in the field of Compound AI Systems. It provides a valuable framework for understanding the integration of LLMs with external components, which goes beyond existing surveys of individual components like RAG or agents. It highlights important design considerations and challenges. While the breadth of the topic limits the depth of analysis in certain areas, and the fast-moving nature of the field will inevitably render parts of the paper obsolete quickly, the paper provides a comprehensive foundation and framework. A score of 7.5 is assigned, indicating a significant, but not exceptional, contribution to the field. The paper provides a valuable contribution, however lacks the novelty and depth to justify a higher score.

### OpenAg: Democratizing Agricultural Intelligence
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.04571v1)
- **Authors**: Srikanth Thudumu, Jason Fisher
- **Abstract**: Agriculture is undergoing a major transformation driven by artificial intelligence (AI), machine learning, and knowledge representation technologies. However, current agricultural intelligence systems often lack contextual understanding, explainability, and adaptability, especially for smallholder farmers with limited resources. General-purpose large language models (LLMs), while powerful, typically lack the domain-specific knowledge and contextual reasoning needed for practical decision support in farming. They tend to produce recommendations that are too generic or unrealistic for real-world applications. To address these challenges, we present OpenAg, a comprehensive framework designed to advance agricultural artificial general intelligence (AGI). OpenAg combines domain-specific foundation models, neural knowledge graphs, multi-agent reasoning, causal explainability, and adaptive transfer learning to deliver context-aware, explainable, and actionable insights. The system includes: (i) a unified agricultural knowledge base that integrates scientific literature, sensor data, and farmer-generated knowledge; (ii) a neural agricultural knowledge graph for structured reasoning and inference; (iii) an adaptive multi-agent reasoning system where AI agents specialize and collaborate across agricultural domains; and (iv) a causal transparency mechanism that ensures AI recommendations are interpretable, scientifically grounded, and aligned with real-world constraints. OpenAg aims to bridge the gap between scientific knowledge and the tacit expertise of experienced farmers to support scalable and locally relevant agricultural decision-making.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces OpenAg, a comprehensive AI framework designed to address the limitations of current agricultural intelligence systems, particularly for smallholder farmers. OpenAg integrates domain-specific foundation models, neural knowledge graphs, multi-agent reasoning, causal explainability, and adaptive transfer learning to provide context-aware, explainable, and actionable insights. Key components include a unified agricultural knowledge base (UAKB), a neural agricultural knowledge graph engine (NAKGG), an adaptive multi-agent reasoning system (AMRS), and a causal agricultural decision transparency mechanism (CADET). The goal is to bridge the gap between scientific knowledge and the tacit expertise of farmers, supporting scalable and locally relevant decision-making. The paper outlines the architecture of OpenAg and details the methodological approaches for its implementation.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper presents a comprehensive, integrated approach to agricultural AI, which combines several established techniques (knowledge graphs, multi-agent systems, transfer learning) into a novel architecture specifically tailored for the agricultural domain. While individual components are not entirely new, the specific combination and adaptation to address the identified challenges in agricultural AI represent a significant step forward. The emphasis on causal explainability and the handling of tacit knowledge are also noteworthy contributions. The idea of building a comprehensive neural knowledge graph for agriculture is a novel undertaking, although similar efforts exist in other domains.

*   **Significance:** The challenges addressed by OpenAg (contextual understanding, explainability, adaptability) are critical for the successful adoption of AI in agriculture, particularly for smallholder farmers. By providing a framework that addresses these limitations, OpenAg has the potential to significantly impact agricultural productivity, sustainability, and resilience. The potential impact of making AI more accessible and trustworthy in agriculture is substantial, especially given the global challenges of food security and climate change. If successfully implemented, OpenAg could lead to more informed decision-making, optimized resource allocation, and improved farming practices.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   Comprehensive approach integrating multiple AI techniques.
    *   Focus on addressing real-world challenges faced by farmers.
    *   Emphasis on explainability and trustworthiness.
    *   Potential for scalability and adaptability across diverse agricultural contexts.
    *   Clearly articulates the limitations of existing approaches and how OpenAg addresses them.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The paper remains largely conceptual.  There's no empirical validation of the proposed architecture or its individual components.  The effectiveness of OpenAg depends heavily on the quality of the UAKB, the performance of the NAKGG, and the ability of the AMRS to generate useful recommendations, all of which require substantial implementation and testing.
    *   The complexity of the architecture may present significant implementation challenges. Integrating diverse data sources, training neural networks, and coordinating multi-agent systems require significant computational resources and expertise.
    *   The paper doesn't provide details about how the specific agricultural ontologies would be created and maintained, nor does it mention the potential role of existing large language models (LLMs) as a source of domain-specific knowledge for integration with the UAKB.
    *   While it mentions cultural adaptation, it could benefit from more detailed discussion of how to ensure OpenAg is culturally appropriate and addresses the specific needs and constraints of farmers in different regions.

**Justification for the Score:**

The paper presents a promising and well-articulated framework for agricultural AI, addressing significant challenges in the field. The integrated approach and the focus on explainability and adaptability are particularly valuable. However, the lack of empirical validation and the potential implementation challenges limit the paper's current impact. The novelty comes from integrating established techniques within a novel architecture tailored for a very specific problem domain.

### Demonstrations of Integrity Attacks in Multi-Agent Systems
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.04572v1)
- **Authors**: Can Zheng, Yuhan Cao, Xiaoning Dong, Tianxing He
- **Abstract**: Large Language Models (LLMs) have demonstrated remarkable capabilities in natural language understanding, code generation, and complex planning. Simultaneously, Multi-Agent Systems (MAS) have garnered attention for their potential to enable cooperation among distributed agents. However, from a multi-party perspective, MAS could be vulnerable to malicious agents that exploit the system to serve self-interests without disrupting its core functionality. This work explores integrity attacks where malicious agents employ subtle prompt manipulation to bias MAS operations and gain various benefits. Four types of attacks are examined: \textit{Scapegoater}, who misleads the system monitor to underestimate other agents' contributions; \textit{Boaster}, who misleads the system monitor to overestimate their own performance; \textit{Self-Dealer}, who manipulates other agents to adopt certain tools; and \textit{Free-Rider}, who hands off its own task to others. We demonstrate that strategically crafted prompts can introduce systematic biases in MAS behavior and executable instructions, enabling malicious agents to effectively mislead evaluation systems and manipulate collaborative agents. Furthermore, our attacks can bypass advanced LLM-based monitors, such as GPT-4o-mini and o3-mini, highlighting the limitations of current detection mechanisms. Our findings underscore the critical need for MAS architectures with robust security protocols and content validation mechanisms, alongside monitoring systems capable of comprehensive risk scenario assessment.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "Demonstrations of Integrity Attacks in Multi-Agent Systems," addressing its novelty and significance:

**Summary:**

The paper presents a systematic analysis of *integrity attacks* in LLM-based Multi-Agent Systems (MAS). It introduces four distinct archetypes of malicious agents:

*   **Scapegoater:** Misleads the system monitor to underestimate other agents' contributions.
*   **Self-Dealer:** Manipulates other agents to adopt certain tools (e.g., biasing search engine usage).
*   **Free-Rider:** Offloads its own assigned tasks to other agents, reducing its resource consumption.
*   **Boaster:** Misleads the system monitor to overestimate their own performance and contribution.

The authors demonstrate that strategically crafted prompts can introduce systematic biases in MAS behavior and executable instructions, enabling malicious agents to effectively mislead evaluation systems and manipulate collaborative agents. They conduct experiments on three MAS architectures (CAMEL, AutoGen, and MetaGPT) across three tasks (code generation, mathematical problem solving, and knowledge-based question answering). A key finding is that these attacks can bypass advanced LLM-based monitors (GPT-4o-mini and GPT-03-mini), highlighting the limitations of current detection mechanisms. The paper emphasizes the need for robust security protocols, content validation, and comprehensive risk scenario assessment in MAS architectures.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Novel Problem Framing:** The paper identifies a relatively unexplored but crucial area of concern in MAS: integrity attacks that undermine fairness and reliability *without* necessarily disrupting system functionality.  This is a more subtle and realistic threat model than direct performance degradation.
    *   **Systematic Analysis:** The four archetypes provide a useful framework for understanding different ways in which malicious agents can manipulate a MAS for their own benefit. The categorization helps in thinking about potential countermeasures.
    *   **Empirical Validation:** The experimental results are compelling, demonstrating the vulnerability of existing MAS architectures to the proposed attacks. The use of multiple MAS frameworks and tasks strengthens the generality of the findings.
    *   **Emphasis on Limitations:** The work is candid about the limitations of current monitoring systems, even when employing advanced LLMs.  The experiments showing the failure of existing LLM monitors provide a concrete motivation for future research.
    *   **Well-Documented:** The paper offers detailed prompt examples and supplementary information in the appendix, improving reproducibility.
*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Attack Sophistication:** While the core concepts are interesting, the presented attacks, while demonstrably effective, seem relatively simple (e.g., hardcoded prompt injections). A significant aspect of future research may need to incorporate adaptive methods that can learn or design more effective attack prompts.

    *   **Generality of Findings:** While the study includes multiple frameworks and tasks, all the experiments are conducted in a specific closed multi agent systems, where the agents communicate within the same model context.
    *   **Defense Strategies:** While the paper highlights the inadequacy of existing monitors, it doesn't offer significant concrete solutions. It acknowledges the complexity, indicating that defenses require far more than simple prompt engineering but is also limited by its experimentation and only tries one defense technique. This limits its immediate impact.
    *   **Evaluation of Automatable Generation:** While the effort to automate the detection is promising it does not fully evaluate the potential capabilities.

*   **Novelty:**  The paper's primary novelty lies in its focus on *integrity attacks*, a specific class of adversarial behavior in MAS that prioritizes manipulation over direct disruption. While adversarial attacks on AI systems are a well-established area, the specific framing within multi-agent collaborative settings, where the objective is to subtly influence behavior and manipulate scoring, is a valuable contribution. Prior works often consider only performance degradation.
*   **Significance:**  The paper is significant because it raises awareness of a critical vulnerability in MAS that could hinder their real-world deployment. As MAS become more prevalent in safety-critical applications (e.g., autonomous driving, healthcare), ensuring the integrity and fairness of their operations is paramount. The findings highlight the limitations of existing LLM-based monitoring systems and motivate further research into robust security mechanisms for MAS. The focus on the economic incentives of the multi agent systems adds another layer of complexity that can be more in align with real world system.

**Score:** 7/10

**Justification:**

The paper makes a solid contribution by identifying and demonstrating a critical vulnerability in MAS that is currently under-addressed.  The systematic analysis and empirical validation strengthen the findings. The 7/10 score reflects the paper's novelty in framing the problem, the significance of the demonstrated vulnerabilities, and the clear limitations in current detection mechanisms. The score is reduced by the lack of novel or advanced defenses in the paper itself. The results are strong in describing what attacks can occur, and this provides great insights into future research for detecting these sorts of attacks and designing countermeasures.


- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### Reasoning or Overthinking: Evaluating Large Language Models on Financial Sentiment Analysis
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.04574v1)
- **Authors**: Dimitris Vamvourellis, Dhagash Mehta
- **Abstract**: We investigate the effectiveness of large language models (LLMs), including reasoning-based and non-reasoning models, in performing zero-shot financial sentiment analysis. Using the Financial PhraseBank dataset annotated by domain experts, we evaluate how various LLMs and prompting strategies align with human-labeled sentiment in a financial context. We compare three proprietary LLMs (GPT-4o, GPT-4.1, o3-mini) under different prompting paradigms that simulate System 1 (fast and intuitive) or System 2 (slow and deliberate) thinking and benchmark them against two smaller models (FinBERT-Prosus, FinBERT-Tone) fine-tuned on financial sentiment analysis. Our findings suggest that reasoning, either through prompting or inherent model design, does not improve performance on this task. Surprisingly, the most accurate and human-aligned combination of model and method was GPT-4o without any Chain-of-Thought (CoT) prompting. We further explore how performance is impacted by linguistic complexity and annotation agreement levels, uncovering that reasoning may introduce overthinking, leading to suboptimal predictions. This suggests that for financial sentiment classification, fast, intuitive "System 1"-like thinking aligns more closely with human judgment compared to "System 2"-style slower, deliberative reasoning simulated by reasoning models or CoT prompting. Our results challenge the default assumption that more reasoning always leads to better LLM decisions, particularly in high-stakes financial applications.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper investigates the effectiveness of large language models (LLMs) in performing financial sentiment analysis, specifically examining whether reasoning-based approaches improve performance compared to simpler, more intuitive methods. The authors compare several LLMs (including proprietary OpenAI models) and prompting strategies that simulate System 1 (fast, intuitive) and System 2 (slow, deliberative) thinking. They use the Financial PhraseBank dataset as a benchmark.  The key finding is counterintuitive: prompting LLMs to engage in explicit reasoning (e.g., Chain-of-Thought prompting) often *reduces* alignment with human-labeled sentiment, especially in low-ambiguity cases. The best results are achieved with GPT-40 using a direct, non-reasoning approach, suggesting that for this task, "System 1" thinking is more aligned with human judgment. The paper also explores the impact of linguistic complexity and annotator agreement on performance.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core finding – that reasoning can *hinder* performance in sentiment analysis – is novel and challenges the conventional wisdom that more reasoning always leads to better LLM decisions.  While some previous work has hinted at CoT's limitations in subjective tasks, this paper provides a clear demonstration of this effect in the financial domain with a systematic comparison of different prompting strategies. The exploration of the dual-process theory analogy adds a valuable framing.
*   **Significance:** Financial sentiment analysis is a practically important task, and understanding how LLMs perform in this domain is valuable. The paper's findings have implications for how LLMs should be deployed in high-stakes financial applications where alignment with human understanding is crucial. Showing that less reasoning can be *more* effective is a significant contribution to the broader understanding of LLM capabilities and limitations. The findings directly address the prevalent assumption that increased reasoning depth universally improves model performance and provides a nuanced perspective on the application of LLMs in subjective judgment tasks. The paper's focus on zero-shot performance is also significant, highlighting inherent reasoning tendencies of models before adaptation through fine-tuning.
*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Comprehensive Evaluation:** The paper conducts a thorough evaluation, comparing several LLMs, including proprietary models, and a range of prompting strategies.
    *   **Clear Research Question:** The research question regarding the impact of reasoning on financial sentiment analysis is well-defined and clearly addressed.
    *   **Dual-Process Framing:** The framing using the dual-process theory from cognitive science provides a useful lens for analyzing the results.
    *   **Analysis of Failure Modes:** The inclusion of an analysis of failure modes provides valuable insights into how different prompting strategies can lead to different kinds of errors.
*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Dataset Limitation:** While Financial PhraseBank is a widely used benchmark, it is also relatively small and curated. A larger and more diverse financial dataset could strengthen the generalizability of the findings.
    *   **Limited Generalization Claims:**  The paper focuses specifically on *financial* sentiment analysis. It acknowledges that the findings may not generalize to other tasks or domains.  A more generalizable model would require analyzing multiple tasks.
    *   **Explanation of "Overthinking":** While the paper identifies "overthinking" as a potential cause of reduced performance, it does not fully explain the underlying mechanisms causing this effect within the LLMs themselves. More in-depth investigation into the internal representations or attention patterns would have strengthened the explanation.
    *   **The black-box nature of LLMs** makes it difficult to pinpoint exactly why reasoning hurts in these cases. This is inherent to the nature of the models, but a discussion of this limitation would be useful.
*   **Potential Influence:** The paper's counterintuitive findings are likely to influence future research on LLM applications, particularly in subjective domains. It encourages a more critical and nuanced approach to reasoning-based prompting and emphasizes the importance of matching prompting strategies to the specific task and goal. The paper also pushes the field to look more closely at when, how, and why reasoning helps *or* hurts in the context of LLMs.

**Score: 8**

**Justification:**

The paper presents a novel and significant finding that challenges the common assumption that more reasoning always improves LLM performance. The findings are well-supported by empirical evidence and framed within a relevant theoretical framework. The paper's thorough evaluation and analysis of failure modes add to its value. While the limitations regarding dataset size, potential generalization, and lack of detailed explanation of internal mechanisms prevent it from being a truly groundbreaking work, its counterintuitive results and clear demonstration of when reasoning is detrimental make it a valuable contribution to the field, warranting a high score. The practical relevance in finance strengthens the impact.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 8/10

### Are LLMs Reliable Translators of Logical Reasoning Across Lexically Diversified Contexts?
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.04575v1)
- **Authors**: Qingchuan Li, Jiatong Li, Zirui Liu, Mingyue Cheng, Yuting Zeng, Qi Liu, Tongxuan Liu
- **Abstract**: Neuro-symbolic approaches combining large language models (LLMs) with solvers excels in logical reasoning problems need long reasoning chains. In this paradigm, LLMs serve as translators, converting natural language reasoning problems into formal logic formulas. Then reliable symbolic solvers return correct solutions. Despite their success, we find that LLMs, as translators, struggle to handle lexical diversification, a common linguistic phenomenon, indicating that LLMs as logic translators are unreliable in real-world scenarios. Moreover, existing logical reasoning benchmarks lack lexical diversity, failing to challenge LLMs' ability to translate such text and thus obscuring this issue. In this work, we propose SCALe, a benchmark designed to address this significant gap through **logic-invariant lexical diversification**. By using LLMs to transform original benchmark datasets into lexically diversified but logically equivalent versions, we evaluate LLMs' ability to consistently map diverse expressions to uniform logical symbols on these new datasets. Experiments using SCALe further confirm that current LLMs exhibit deficiencies in this capability. Building directly on the deficiencies identified through our benchmark, we propose a new method, MenTaL, to address this limitation. This method guides LLMs to first construct a table unifying diverse expressions before performing translation. Applying MenTaL through in-context learning and supervised fine-tuning (SFT) significantly improves the performance of LLM translators on lexically diversified text. Our code is now available at https://github.com/wufeiwuwoshihua/LexicalDiver.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "Are LLMs Reliable Translators of Logical Reasoning Across Lexically Diversified Contexts?":

**Summary:**

The paper identifies a crucial weakness in the application of Large Language Models (LLMs) to neuro-symbolic reasoning tasks: their unreliability in translating natural language into formal logic when faced with lexical diversity (multiple ways of expressing the same logical concept). Existing benchmarks, focused on logical structure, fail to adequately test this translation ability. The authors propose SCALe, a novel benchmark designed to assess Semantic Consistency Mapping Ability through Logic-invariant Lexical Diversification. Using LLMs to transform existing benchmark datasets into lexically diversified versions, they demonstrate that LLMs struggle to map diverse expressions to consistent logical symbols.  To address this, they introduce MenTaL, a framework that guides LLMs to construct a mental representation table unifying diverse expressions before translation.  Through in-context learning and supervised fine-tuning, MenTaL significantly improves LLM translator performance on lexically diversified text.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's primary novelty lies in identifying and addressing the problem of "semantic consistency mapping" in the context of LLMs as translators for logical reasoning. While neuro-symbolic approaches and LLM reasoning have been extensively studied, this specific aspect of lexical diversity has been relatively overlooked. The introduction of SCALe as a targeted benchmark is a significant contribution, providing a concrete way to measure and analyze this weakness. The MenTaL framework, while building on cognitive science principles, offers a practical solution tailored to the observed deficiencies of LLMs.

*   **Significance:** The paper's significance stems from the growing importance of reliable LLM-tool interactions. As LLMs are increasingly integrated with external solvers and tools, their ability to consistently interpret natural language becomes crucial for system robustness and scalability. By highlighting the limitations in semantic consistency mapping, the paper points to a critical area for improvement in LLM-based reasoning systems.  MenTaL offers a promising initial approach to bridge this gap, potentially enabling more reliable and adaptable logical reasoning applications. The open-sourcing of SCALe and MenTaL further enhances the paper's impact by providing valuable resources for the research community.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Clearly defined problem:** The paper articulates the problem of semantic consistency mapping very clearly and convincingly.
    *   **Well-designed benchmark:** SCALe is a methodologically sound benchmark, directly addressing the identified gap in existing evaluations. The logic-invariant lexical diversification approach is clever and effective.
    *   **Effective solution:** The MenTaL framework demonstrates a tangible improvement in LLM performance on the task. Both in-context learning and fine-tuning results are promising.
    *   **Comprehensive Evaluation:** The paper provides a thorough experimental evaluation, including ablation studies (diversification intensity), error analyses, and comparisons across multiple LLMs.
    *   **Reproducibility:** The open-source release of the code is a major strength, promoting reproducibility and further research in this area.
    *   **Addresses a gap in literature**: Points out that prior works focused on translation methods and accuracy improvements but did not address the issues of mapping synonymous concepts to consistent logical symbols

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Limited Diversification Control:** The method for creating SCALe relies on LLMs, and might be hard to control.
    *   **Limited generalizability of SCALe:** Although the methodology is generalizable, the SCALe benchmark is focused on logical reasoning and the impact to other domains of LLM-tool interactions might vary.
    *   **Potential for over-optimization:** Given the focus on a specific type of lexical diversity, there's a risk that MenTaL is optimized for this particular challenge and may not generalize to other types of language variation. While the paper acknowledges such limitations, further investigation is warranted.
    *   **Complexity of MenTaL*:** The dynamic maintenance of the table in the MentaL* requires a lot of additional rules and might not be very efficient to apply or scale.
    *   **May need task-specific tuning**: While SFT was conducted on three dataset tasks and solver formats and showed generalizability, more generalizability experiments can always be conducted.

*   **Potential Influence:** This paper has the potential to influence the development of more robust and reliable LLM-based reasoning systems. The SCALe benchmark could become a standard evaluation tool for assessing semantic consistency mapping abilities. The MenTaL framework, or variations of it, could be incorporated into future LLM architectures or training methodologies. Additionally, it raises awareness to the limitations that LLMs have in translating synonymous concepts into appropriate symbols.

**Score: 8**

**Rationale:** The paper addresses a significant and previously underappreciated problem in LLM-based reasoning, offers a solid benchmark (SCALe) for measuring this problem, and proposes a practical solution (MenTaL) that demonstrably improves performance. The thorough evaluation and open-sourcing further contribute to its value. While some concerns exist about the generalizability of the solution and the method for creating SCALe, the overall contribution is substantial, warranting a high score.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 8/10

### Selecting Demonstrations for Many-Shot In-Context Learning via Gradient Matching
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.04579v1)
- **Authors**: Jianfei Zhang, Bei Li, Jun Bai, Rumei Li, Yanmeng Wang, Chenghua Lin, Wenge Rong
- **Abstract**: In-Context Learning (ICL) empowers Large Language Models (LLMs) for rapid task adaptation without Fine-Tuning (FT), but its reliance on demonstration selection remains a critical challenge. While many-shot ICL shows promising performance through scaled demonstrations, the selection method for many-shot demonstrations remains limited to random selection in existing work. Since the conventional instance-level retrieval is not suitable for many-shot scenarios, we hypothesize that the data requirements for in-context learning and fine-tuning are analogous. To this end, we introduce a novel gradient matching approach that selects demonstrations by aligning fine-tuning gradients between the entire training set of the target task and the selected examples, so as to approach the learning effect on the entire training set within the selected examples. Through gradient matching on relatively small models, e.g., Qwen2.5-3B or Llama3-8B, our method consistently outperforms random selection on larger LLMs from 4-shot to 128-shot scenarios across 9 diverse datasets. For instance, it surpasses random selection by 4% on Qwen2.5-72B and Llama3-70B, and by around 2% on 5 closed-source LLMs. This work unlocks more reliable and effective many-shot ICL, paving the way for its broader application.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper addresses the challenge of demonstration selection in many-shot in-context learning (ICL). It proposes a novel approach called Curriculum Latent Gradient (CLG) matching, which selects demonstrations by aligning the fine-tuning gradients of the entire training set with those of the selected examples. The key idea is that demonstrations should guide the language model's learning process in a similar way to how it would be fine-tuned on the entire dataset. The method involves latent concept learning on a small language model, followed by gradient calculation and a greedy search/local optimization to choose the demonstration set.  The paper demonstrates that CLG consistently outperforms random selection and other baselines across a diverse range of datasets and language models, including both open-source and closed-source LLMs.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:**  The core idea of using gradient matching for demonstration selection is novel in the context of ICL. The existing literature primarily focuses on similarity-based retrieval or reinforcement learning. Framing demonstration selection as a learning problem and leveraging gradient information provides a new perspective. The Curriculum Learning aspect adds another layer, capturing the learning trajectory rather than just a single point. However, the connection to dataset condensation, while mentioned, could be further explored and clarified.

* **Significance:** Improving many-shot ICL is significant because it allows for better task adaptation without fine-tuning, which is resource-intensive. The paper's ability to achieve consistent performance gains across different LLMs, including closed-source ones, highlights the practicality and transferability of the proposed method. This can enable wider adoption of ICL in real-world applications where computational resources and model weights may be constrained. The improvements shown justify the increased pre-processing computational cost. The analysis of the impact of various factors on CLG like diversity and coverage adds to the understanding of its advantages.

* **Strengths:**
    * **Strong Empirical Results:** The paper presents comprehensive experimental results across multiple datasets, tasks, and models, demonstrating the effectiveness of CLG compared to various baselines.
    * **Transferability:**  A key strength is the ability of CLG-selected demonstrations to transfer well to different LLM architectures, particularly closed-source models where fine-tuning is often restricted.
    * **Ablation Study:** The ablation study clearly shows the importance of the gradient matching aspect.
    * **Analysis of Diversity/Coverage:** The paper addresses concerns of diversity and coverage by demonstrating that CLG maintains better label distributions compared to random selection and other methods.
    * **Scalability:**  The computational cost of CLG is shown to be manageable.

* **Weaknesses:**
    * **Dependency on a Small LM:** The method relies on training a small language model to compute gradients, which introduces a dependency on the quality of this model. While experiments use Qwen2.5-3B/Llama3-8B which are solid models, further research could explore how the choice of the smaller LM impacts the demonstration selection process. What if the target model architecture significantly differs from the small LM, such as in MoE models or a very small model fine-tuned with a different objective?
    * **Greedy Search:** While practical, the greedy search for demonstration selection may not find the absolute optimal set. The local optimization phase helps, but there's a trade-off between computational cost and optimality.  Are there ways to make the optimization more robust, or explore more advanced methods to find the right set of training examples?
    * **Limited Analysis of Negative Results:** The paper mentions that BM25-Major and Latent-Bayesian perform worse than Random, yet the discussion around the limitations of these methods feels somewhat brief. While explanation is offered, additional analysis into _why_ they fail would be beneficial to truly understand the CLG's superiority.

* **Impact:** The paper has the potential to significantly impact how demonstrations are selected for many-shot ICL, especially in scenarios where computational resources are limited or fine-tuning is not feasible. It provides a novel approach that balances performance and efficiency and offers insights into the learning dynamics of ICL.

**Justification for Score:**

Considering the novelty of the approach, strong empirical results, transferability, and the potential to improve many-shot ICL in real-world scenarios, but also acknowledging its dependencies on a small LM, and greedy search optimization, and limited negative results, a score of 7.5 is warranted. The paper provides a significant contribution to the field by offering a practical and effective method for demonstration selection that is not tied to the specific architecture or availability of the target LLM.

Score: 7.5

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### LESS: Large Language Model Enhanced Semi-Supervised Learning for Speech Foundational Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.04586v1)
- **Authors**: Wen Ding, Fan Qian
- **Abstract**: We introduce LESS (Large Language Model Enhanced Semi-supervised Learning), a versatile framework that leverages Large Language Models (LLMs) to correct pseudo labels generated from in-the-wild data. Within the LESS framework, pseudo-labeled text from Automatic Speech Recognition (ASR) or Automatic Speech Translation (AST) of the unsupervised data is refined by an LLM, and augmented by a data filtering strategy to optimize LLM knowledge transfer efficiency. Experiments on both Mandarin ASR and Spanish-to-English AST tasks show that LESS achieves a notable absolute WER reduction of 3.77% on the Wenet Speech test set, as well as BLEU scores of 34.0 and 64.7 on Callhome and Fisher test sets respectively. These results validate the adaptability of LESS across different languages, tasks, and domains. Ablation studies conducted with various LLMs and prompt configurations provide novel insights into leveraging LLM-derived knowledge for speech processing applications.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces LESS (Large Language Model Enhanced Semi-supervised Learning), a framework designed to improve the quality of pseudo-labels used in semi-supervised learning for speech foundational models (SFMs). LESS leverages large language models (LLMs) to correct pseudo-labels generated from in-the-wild data by ASR or AST models, and then uses data filtering to improve the efficiency of knowledge transfer from the LLM to the SFM. Experiments on Mandarin ASR and Spanish-to-English AST show improvements in WER and BLEU scores, respectively, demonstrating the adaptability of LESS across different languages and tasks.  Ablation studies explore various LLMs and prompt configurations.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core idea of using LLMs to refine pseudo-labels in speech processing tasks isn't entirely new. Previous work ([19], [20]) has explored similar approaches, though typically with smaller student models and often relying on curated data. The novelty lies in the application of this approach to large-scale SFMs and the emphasis on utilizing *in-the-wild* data.  The combination of LLM-based correction with a specific data filtering strategy contributes incremental novelty.  However, the paper doesn't introduce a fundamentally groundbreaking technique.

*   **Significance:** The significance stems from the demonstrated effectiveness of LESS in improving performance on both ASR and AST tasks, particularly in leveraging abundant, but often noisy, in-the-wild data.  The improvements shown on the Wenet Speech test set and Callhome/Fisher datasets are meaningful. The ablations provide insights into the impact of different LLMs and prompt configurations, which can inform future research. The results underscore the potential of LLMs as valuable tools for enhancing speech processing, particularly in low-resource scenarios or when dealing with noisy data.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Empirical Validation:** The paper provides strong empirical evidence to support its claims, with experiments on diverse tasks (ASR and AST) and languages (Mandarin and Spanish/English).
    *   **Practical Applicability:** The LESS framework is relatively straightforward to implement and can be easily integrated into existing semi-supervised learning pipelines.
    *   **Ablation Studies:** The thorough ablation studies provide valuable insights into the influence of different LLMs and prompt strategies.
    *   **Use of in-the-wild Data:** Focus on in-the-wild data is a plus since labeled speech data can be expensive to obtain, making it more readily applicable to real-world scenarios.
*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Incremental Advance:** While the results are positive, the approach builds upon existing techniques, leading to an incremental rather than revolutionary advance.
    *   **Computational Cost:**  The use of LLMs adds a considerable computational cost to the training pipeline, which could be a barrier to adoption in resource-constrained environments. The paper does not adequately address the computational overhead.
    *   **Lack of Deeper Analysis:** The paper lacks in-depth error analysis to understand the types of errors the LLM is correcting and why certain LLMs and prompts are more effective than others. A qualitative analysis of successful and unsuccessful LLM corrections could offer further insights.
    *   **Limited Iterations:** The number of iterations of the full pipeline is limited in some experiments. Further iterating might reveal if the improvements eventually plateau or degrade.
    *   **Hyperparameter Sensitivity:** The paper mentions the `hypo_wer` threshold but doesn't fully explore its sensitivity.  Optimal settings may depend on the specific dataset and LLM used.
    *   **Reproducibility Concerns:** While the authors state that the code will be made open source, the details of some implementation choices (e.g. exact prompts, specific configurations for the deepspeed) are unclear, which is a concern for reproducibility before the code is released.
*   **Potential Influence:** The paper can influence future research by encouraging the development of more effective LLM-based techniques for semi-supervised speech processing. The insights from the ablation studies can guide the selection of appropriate LLMs and prompt strategies for specific tasks. The focus on in-the-wild data can help to improve the robustness of SFMs in real-world scenarios.

**Overall:**

The paper presents a useful extension of existing semi-supervised learning techniques for speech processing. The demonstrated improvements on various tasks and languages, coupled with the insightful ablation studies, contribute valuable knowledge to the field.  However, the incremental nature of the advance and the lack of in-depth analysis limit its overall impact. The computational cost implications are also a concern.

Score: 6.5

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 6/10

### Safe: Enhancing Mathematical Reasoning in Large Language Models via Retrospective Step-aware Formal Verification
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.04592v1)
- **Authors**: Chengwu Liu, Ye Yuan, Yichun Yin, Yan Xu, Xin Xu, Zaoyu Chen, Yasheng Wang, Lifeng Shang, Qun Liu, Ming Zhang
- **Abstract**: Chain-of-Thought (CoT) prompting has become the de facto method to elicit reasoning capabilities from large language models (LLMs). However, to mitigate hallucinations in CoT that are notoriously difficult to detect, current methods such as process reward models (PRMs) or self-consistency operate as opaque boxes and do not provide checkable evidence for their judgments, possibly limiting their effectiveness. To address this issue, we draw inspiration from the idea that "the gold standard for supporting a mathematical claim is to provide a proof". We propose a retrospective, step-aware formal verification framework $Safe$. Rather than assigning arbitrary scores, we strive to articulate mathematical claims in formal mathematical language Lean 4 at each reasoning step and provide formal proofs to identify hallucinations. We evaluate our framework $Safe$ across multiple language models and various mathematical datasets, demonstrating a significant performance improvement while offering interpretable and verifiable evidence. We also propose $FormalStep$ as a benchmark for step correctness theorem proving with $30,809$ formal statements. To the best of our knowledge, our work represents the first endeavor to utilize formal mathematical language Lean 4 for verifying natural language content generated by LLMs, aligning with the reason why formal mathematical languages were created in the first place: to provide a robust foundation for hallucination-prone human-written proofs.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces Safe, a novel framework designed to enhance the mathematical reasoning capabilities of large language models (LLMs) by incorporating retrospective, step-aware formal verification.  Instead of relying on opaque box methods like process reward models (PRMs) or self-consistency, Safe articulates mathematical claims in the formal language Lean 4 at each reasoning step and attempts to provide formal proofs to identify hallucinations. The framework decomposes complex reasoning trajectories into simpler steps, leverages LLMs for auto-formalization of these steps into Lean 4 statements, and then uses automated theorem provers to verify these statements. The verification states are aggregated to produce a retrospective score, which is then combined with a prospective score from an off-the-shelf PRM. The authors also contribute FormalStep, a new benchmark dataset for step correctness theorem proving, comprising 30,809 formal statements. The experiments demonstrate significant performance improvements using Safe across multiple LLMs and mathematical datasets, offering interpretable and verifiable evidence of correctness.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The key novelty lies in the integration of formal verification using Lean 4 into the chain-of-thought reasoning process of LLMs.  While auto-formalization and automated theorem proving are established areas, their application *within* the CoT loop *specifically for hallucination mitigation and explainable verification* is a significant contribution. The creation of FormalStep is also a valuable addition, providing a specific resource for this type of research.  The combination of retrospective formal verification with prospective PRMs is another aspect of novelty.

*   **Significance:** The paper addresses a critical problem: the difficulty of detecting hallucinations in LLM reasoning, especially in domains like mathematics. By providing a checkable evidence through formal proofs, the approach aims to improve trust and reliability in LLM outputs. The improved performance reported in the experiments suggests the potential of Safe to be a useful tool for enhancing LLMs.  The interpretability offered by the framework is a major advantage over "black box" approaches.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   Well-defined problem and clear motivation.
    *   Sound methodology that combines multiple techniques in a novel way.
    *   Empirical validation across different LLMs and datasets, demonstrating consistent improvements.
    *   The interpretability of the approach enhances trust in the reasoning process.
    *   Creation of a valuable new dataset, FormalStep, for step-wise formal verification.
    *  The paper is well-written and structured.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The reliance on GPT-40 for auto-formalization can introduce bias and limit the practicality in resource-constrained settings. There is a need to explore more efficient and open-source alternatives.
    *   The computational cost of the formal verification process could be a barrier to adoption, especially for large-scale applications. The authors acknowledge this limitation and identify it as a direction for future work.  The 4-8x higher cost than PRMs needs to be considered.
    *   The correlation between Lean 4 REPL outputs and final evaluation scores is considered indirect, and the LSTM scoring mechanism lacks perfect interpretability.
    *   While the combination with PRMs is promising, the synergistic effect is not overwhelmingly large. There could be more investigation into different combination strategies.
    *   The experiments also suggest limited improvement on datasets where LLMs are already performing near saturation, indicating that the approach might be less effective for easier problems.
    *   The scope of Lean 4 might limit application to specific domains.

*   **Potential Impact:** The paper has the potential to influence the development of more reliable and trustworthy LLMs for mathematical reasoning. The approach could be extended to other domains where formal verification is possible (e.g., code generation). The FormalStep dataset will likely be a valuable resource for future research in this area.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper presents a solid contribution with a novel approach to addressing a significant problem. The empirical results are promising, and the inclusion of interpretable verification steps is a key advantage. However, the computational cost, reliance on a closed model (GPT-4o for auto-formalization), and somewhat limited synergistic effect with PRMs prevent it from reaching the highest score. The identified limitations provide clear directions for future research.

Score: 7.5

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### A MISMATCHED Benchmark for Scientific Natural Language Inference
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.04603v1)
- **Authors**: Firoz Shaik, Mobashir Sadat, Nikita Gautam, Doina Caragea, Cornelia Caragea
- **Abstract**: Scientific Natural Language Inference (NLI) is the task of predicting the semantic relation between a pair of sentences extracted from research articles. Existing datasets for this task are derived from various computer science (CS) domains, whereas non-CS domains are completely ignored. In this paper, we introduce a novel evaluation benchmark for scientific NLI, called MISMATCHED. The new MISMATCHED benchmark covers three non-CS domains-PSYCHOLOGY, ENGINEERING, and PUBLIC HEALTH, and contains 2,700 human annotated sentence pairs. We establish strong baselines on MISMATCHED using both Pre-trained Small Language Models (SLMs) and Large Language Models (LLMs). Our best performing baseline shows a Macro F1 of only 78.17% illustrating the substantial headroom for future improvements. In addition to introducing the MISMATCHED benchmark, we show that incorporating sentence pairs having an implicit scientific NLI relation between them in model training improves their performance on scientific NLI. We make our dataset and code publicly available on GitHub.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces a new benchmark dataset called MISMATCHED for scientific Natural Language Inference (NLI).  This dataset is designed to evaluate the out-of-domain robustness of scientific NLI models, as it contains sentence pairs sourced from three non-computer science domains: Psychology, Engineering, and Public Health.  The authors establish strong baselines on the MISMATCHED dataset using both small and large language models (SLMs and LLMs). They show that current models struggle on this dataset, illustrating the need for improvements in out-of-domain generalization.  Additionally, they demonstrate that incorporating sentence pairs with implicit scientific NLI relations into model training improves performance. The dataset and code are made publicly available.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core novelty lies in the creation of the MISMATCHED dataset. Existing scientific NLI datasets primarily focus on computer science domains, limiting their ability to assess the generalizability of models to other scientific fields. The inclusion of Psychology, Engineering, and Public Health represents a valuable extension. The exploration of implicit relations and their effect on performance also contributes to novelty.

*   **Significance:** The significance is twofold:

    1.  **Benchmarking:** MISMATCHED provides a more challenging and realistic benchmark for evaluating the performance of scientific NLI models in real-world scenarios where data may come from diverse scientific disciplines.  This is crucial for applications where NLI models are used to extract knowledge or assist in research across scientific boundaries. The relatively poor performance of current baselines on the MISMATCHED dataset highlights a significant gap in the capabilities of existing models.

    2.  **Implicit Relations:** The demonstration that incorporating implicit relations (adjacent sentences with NLI relationships not explicitly signaled by linking phrases) can improve model performance is a valuable insight. This suggests that capturing contextual information and inferential abilities beyond explicit linking is important for scientific NLI.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Well-defined Problem:** The paper addresses a clear and important problem: the lack of out-of-domain evaluation for scientific NLI.
    *   **Dataset Creation:** The dataset construction process is well-documented, including data sources, annotation procedures, and statistics.  The use of human annotators and consideration of agreement rates are positive aspects.
    *   **Thorough Evaluation:** The baselines are comprehensive, encompassing both SLMs and LLMs. The analysis of the "Reasoning" relation is insightful. The experiments on implicit relations further strengthens the findings.
    *   **Public Availability:** Making the dataset and code publicly available enhances reproducibility and encourages further research.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Limited Scope of Non-CS Domains:** While the inclusion of Psychology, Engineering, and Public Health is a good start, the dataset still represents only a small subset of the vast array of scientific disciplines. Future work could explore a wider range of scientific fields.
    *   **Potential Bias in Implicit Relation Extraction:** While the paper tries to address this, there may be inherent biases in the pseudo-labeling and confidence filtering method used to create implicit NLI pairs. The model used for generating pseudo labels might impose its own biases on the extracted dataset.
    *   **Limited LLM Evaluation.** Evaluation is done on only zero-shot for some LLMs, while others use few-shot approaches. A unified evaluation schema across all models would improve comparability and provide a more comprehensive overview of LLM performance.
    *   **No In-Depth Error Analysis** A more detailed error analysis, delving into the types of mistakes the models make on the MISMATCHED data, would provide valuable insights for future research directions. For example, understanding specific patterns in the data that cause models to fail could help develop targeted strategies for improving robustness and generalization.
    *  **Absence of domain adaptation methods** There is no examination of any domain adaptation methods that could potentially improve performance in out-of-domain scientific NLI tasks.

*   **Potential Influence:**  The paper is likely to influence the direction of research in scientific NLI by:

    *   Encouraging the development of more robust and generalizable models.
    *   Inspiring the creation of new datasets that cover a broader range of scientific domains.
    *   Promoting the exploration of implicit relations and contextual reasoning in NLI.

*   **Justification for Score:** The paper makes a valuable contribution by creating a new benchmark and highlighting the need for improved out-of-domain generalization in scientific NLI. While it has some limitations, its strengths in dataset creation, thorough evaluation, and insights on implicit relations outweigh its weaknesses. The paper's potential influence on future research makes it a significant contribution.

**Score: 7.5**

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### SmartAvatar: Text- and Image-Guided Human Avatar Generation with VLM AI Agents
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.04606v1)
