Also, the improvement that human-based OCR creates (the "OCR by Human" results) makes the case that VLM is not currently advanced enough to be implemented. Finally, while acknowledging a preference for augmented AI in education, they do not explore if this implementation could be used alongside or in conjunction with Indonesian teachers.

Despite these limitations, the paper offers valuable contributions to the field. It highlights the need for culturally sensitive AI tools in education and provides empirical evidence of the challenges and opportunities in applying VLMs and LLMs in under-resourced settings. The dataset and the detailed analysis will likely serve as a benchmark for future research in this area. By providing a practical experiment focused on the Indonesian education system, and focusing on the limitations of the existing models, the authors have laid groundwork for further research in this space, which could lead to greater integration into education systems that have historically lacked the resources for it.

Score: 7.0

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### DualX-VSR: Dual Axial Spatial$\times$Temporal Transformer for Real-World Video Super-Resolution without Motion Compensation
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.04830v1)
- **Authors**: Shuo Cao, Yihao Liu, Xiaohui Li. Yuanting Gao. Yu Zhou, Chao Dong
- **Abstract**: Transformer-based models like ViViT and TimeSformer have advanced video understanding by effectively modeling spatiotemporal dependencies. Recent video generation models, such as Sora and Vidu, further highlight the power of transformers in long-range feature extraction and holistic spatiotemporal modeling. However, directly applying these models to real-world video super-resolution (VSR) is challenging, as VSR demands pixel-level precision, which can be compromised by tokenization and sequential attention mechanisms. While recent transformer-based VSR models attempt to address these issues using smaller patches and local attention, they still face limitations such as restricted receptive fields and dependence on optical flow-based alignment, which can introduce inaccuracies in real-world settings. To overcome these issues, we propose Dual Axial Spatial$\times$Temporal Transformer for Real-World Video Super-Resolution (DualX-VSR), which introduces a novel dual axial spatial$\times$temporal attention mechanism that integrates spatial and temporal information along orthogonal directions. DualX-VSR eliminates the need for motion compensation, offering a simplified structure that provides a cohesive representation of spatiotemporal information. As a result, DualX-VSR achieves high fidelity and superior performance in real-world VSR task.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces DualX-VSR, a novel video super-resolution (VSR) approach that leverages a dual axial spatial-temporal transformer.  Unlike existing transformer-based VSR methods, DualX-VSR eliminates the need for motion compensation by employing a dual axial attention mechanism. This mechanism integrates spatial and temporal information along orthogonal directions (vertical-temporal and horizontal-temporal). This avoids the sequential processing of space and time typical in other models and simplifies the architecture. The authors show that DualX-VSR achieves high fidelity and superior performance in real-world VSR tasks, outperforming methods that rely on optical flow alignment or local attention.  The approach also maintains a relatively simple architecture inherited from ViViT, avoiding complex auxiliary modules. The paper details the architecture, training strategy (pretrain and finetune), and provides extensive experimental results comparing DualX-VSR against various state-of-the-art methods.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The primary novelty lies in the dual axial spatial-temporal attention mechanism.  While axial attention itself isn't entirely new, its application to VSR in a way that inherently integrates spatial and temporal information *simultaneously* and *without* explicit motion compensation is a significant contribution. This bypasses the common issues associated with flow-based alignment and limitations of local attention. The idea to decompose the 3D video tensor into orthogonal dimensions (height-time and width-time) for attention calculation is also a relatively original and practically motivated approach for reducing computational complexity. This design aspect is novel compared to existing VSR methods.

*   **Significance:** The significance stems from the potential to improve real-world VSR performance by addressing limitations of flow-based methods, which are prone to errors under realistic degradation. The simpler architecture achieved by removing motion compensation leads to increased robustness and computational efficiency. The experiments demonstrate that DualX-VSR outperforms existing methods on several datasets, and critically is compared to VIVIT-VSR a model utilizing the same fundamental structure but not utilizing the novel attention mechanism proposed. It is also specifically tested for temporal consistency with strong results. Further, the detailed ablation studies help analyze different parts of the network for their impact. The paper's focus on real-world scenarios makes it particularly relevant.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   Well-defined problem and clear motivation.
    *   The dual axial spatial-temporal attention mechanism is novel and effective.
    *   Ablation studies provide insights into the impact of different components.
    *   Extensive experimental results on both synthetic and real-world datasets.
    *   Addresses limitations of existing methods based on flow or local attention.
    *    Strong temporal consistency results.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   While the paper compares against several SotA models, the evaluation of specific "zero shot" super resolution performance is not included.
    *   The method is highly reliant on transformer architecture, which are known to be computationally expensive.
    *   The paper only explores the effects of two different attention block connection types, serial and interleaved. It would be beneficial to explore more connection types.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence future research in VSR by promoting the development of motion-compensation-free methods and by demonstrating the effectiveness of axial attention for spatiotemporal integration. The simplified architecture of DualX-VSR makes it easier to implement and extend, which could lead to widespread adoption in real-world applications. The performance boost compared to existing state-of-the-art architectures is considerable.

**Score: 7.5**

**Justification:** The paper presents a novel and effective approach to real-world video super-resolution. The dual axial spatial-temporal attention mechanism and the elimination of motion compensation are significant contributions. The experimental results are compelling, and the ablation studies provide valuable insights. The paper is well-written and easy to understand. However, there remains room to explore further improvements by scaling the architecture, and to address issues with zero shot super resolution performance. Although these limitations are considerable, the paper still makes a notable advancement in the field of video super-resolution.

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 7/10

### Joint Evaluation of Answer and Reasoning Consistency for Hallucination Detection in Large Reasoning Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.04832v1)
- **Authors**: Changyue Wang, Weihang Su, Qingyao Ai, Yiqun Liu
- **Abstract**: Large Reasoning Models (LRMs) extend large language models with explicit, multi-step reasoning traces to enhance transparency and performance on complex tasks. However, these reasoning traces can be redundant or logically inconsistent, making them a new source of hallucination that is difficult to detect. Existing hallucination detection methods focus primarily on answer-level uncertainty and often fail to detect hallucinations or logical inconsistencies arising from the model's reasoning trace. This oversight is particularly problematic for LRMs, where the explicit thinking trace is not only an important support to the model's decision-making process but also a key source of potential hallucination. To this end, we propose RACE (Reasoning and Answer Consistency Evaluation), a novel framework specifically tailored for hallucination detection in LRMs. RACE operates by extracting essential reasoning steps and computing four diagnostic signals: inter-sample consistency of reasoning traces, entropy-based answer uncertainty, semantic alignment between reasoning and answers, and internal coherence of reasoning. This joint analysis enables fine-grained hallucination detection even when the final answer appears correct. Experiments across datasets and different LLMs demonstrate that RACE outperforms existing hallucination detection baselines, offering a robust and generalizable solution for evaluating LRMs. Our code is available at: https://github.com/bebr2/RACE.
- **Summary**: Okay, here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces RACE (Reasoning and Answer Consistency Evaluation), a novel black-box framework for detecting hallucinations in Large Reasoning Models (LRMs).  RACE jointly evaluates the reasoning trace and the final answer, moving beyond traditional answer-level uncertainty measures. It decomposes hallucination detection into four complementary components: (1) reasoning path consistency (inter-sample coherence of reasoning traces), (2) answer uncertainty (semantic entropy of the answer space), (3) reasoning-answer alignment (whether reasoning supports the answer), and (4) reasoning internal coherence (measuring speculative content within the reasoning trace). A CoT Extraction module distills key reasoning steps to mitigate noise.  Experiments across various datasets and LLMs demonstrate that RACE outperforms existing hallucination detection baselines.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's primary novelty lies in its *holistic approach to hallucination detection in LRMs*.  Existing methods tend to focus primarily on answer-level uncertainty, overlooking the potential for inconsistencies and "hallucinations" within the reasoning trace itself. RACE's decomposition into the four complementary modules is a valuable contribution, allowing for a more nuanced understanding of where and how hallucinations manifest. The CoT Extraction module also helps to reduce noise and improve the accuracy of hallucination detection. While the individual components (e.g., using semantic entropy for answer uncertainty) are not entirely new, their *integration into a unified framework specifically tailored for LRMs is a key strength*.
*   **Significance:**  As LRMs become increasingly prevalent, ensuring their reliability and trustworthiness is paramount. This paper addresses a significant challenge: detecting subtle forms of hallucination that are difficult to identify using traditional methods. If RACE proves to be a reliable and generalizable tool, it could have a significant impact on the development and deployment of safer and more trustworthy LRMs. The black-box nature of RACE also makes it highly practical, as it does not require access to the model's internal states.
*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Holistic Approach:** RACE considers both reasoning and answer consistency, providing a more comprehensive assessment of hallucination risk.
    *   **Practicality:** It's designed as a black-box method, making it applicable to a wide range of LRMs without requiring model-specific modifications.
    *   **Strong Empirical Results:** The paper presents extensive experimental results across multiple datasets and models, demonstrating the effectiveness of RACE compared to existing baselines.
    *   **Clear Formulation:** The information-theoretic formulation provides a solid foundation for the design of the framework.
*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Linear Combination of Metrics:** The final score aggregation uses a simple linear combination with equal weights. While justified by empirical simplicity, a more sophisticated weighting scheme (perhaps learned from data) could potentially improve performance.
    *   **CoT Extractor Reliance:** The performance of RACE depends on the quality of the CoT Extraction module. The synthetic training data generation process, while well-motivated, could introduce biases or limitations.
    *   **Computational Cost:** While the paper presents an efficiency analysis, the CoT extraction and step-by-step comparison in reasoning consistency component will add some computational cost compared to purely answer-based methods.
    *   **Limited Scope**: The paper focuses solely on factual hallucinations, neglecting other types of hallucinations like those related to logical consistency or reasoning errors.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence future research on hallucination detection in LRMs. The RACE framework provides a valuable starting point for developing more sophisticated and nuanced methods. The emphasis on reasoning consistency is particularly important and could inspire new techniques for evaluating and improving the reliability of LRMs.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper makes a strong contribution by providing a principled and practical approach for detecting hallucinations in LRMs. The holistic approach, the practical black-box nature, and the solid empirical results warrant a high score. However, the limitations related to the linear combination of metrics, CoT extractor reliance, and neglect of logical hallucination somewhat temper the overall impact. The paper effectively addresses a critical issue in the field of LRMs and has the potential to advance future research in this area.

- **Score**: 8/10

### On Automating Security Policies with Contemporary LLMs
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.04838v1)
- **Authors**: Pablo Fernández Saura, K. R. Jayaram, Vatche Isahagian, Jorge Bernal Bernabé, Antonio Skarmeta
- **Abstract**: The complexity of modern computing environments and the growing sophistication of cyber threats necessitate a more robust, adaptive, and automated approach to security enforcement. In this paper, we present a framework leveraging large language models (LLMs) for automating attack mitigation policy compliance through an innovative combination of in-context learning and retrieval-augmented generation (RAG). We begin by describing how our system collects and manages both tool and API specifications, storing them in a vector database to enable efficient retrieval of relevant information. We then detail the architectural pipeline that first decomposes high-level mitigation policies into discrete tasks and subsequently translates each task into a set of actionable API calls. Our empirical evaluation, conducted using publicly available CTI policies in STIXv2 format and Windows API documentation, demonstrates significant improvements in precision, recall, and F1-score when employing RAG compared to a non-RAG baseline.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces a framework for automating security policy enforcement, specifically focusing on attack mitigation, by leveraging Large Language Models (LLMs). It combines in-context learning and Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) to translate high-level security policies into executable API calls. The system first decomposes complex policies into discrete tasks using one LLM and then translates each task into API calls using another LLM augmented with relevant tool and API documentation retrieved from a vector database.  The authors empirically evaluate their approach using publicly available Cyber Threat Intelligence (CTI) policies and Windows API documentation, demonstrating improved precision, recall, and F1-score compared to a non-RAG baseline.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Strengths:**

    * **Novelty in Integration:** The paper's core strength lies in its integration of existing technologies (LLMs, RAG, vector databases) into a cohesive framework specifically designed for automated security policy enforcement. While each component is not new, the combination addresses a relevant problem in a practical manner.
    * **Practical Focus:** The focus on attack mitigation for Windows systems grounded the research in reality. The use of real-world CTI policies (STIXv2 format) and Windows API documentation adds credibility and demonstrates applicability.
    * **Empirical Validation:** The evaluation using precision, recall, and F1-score provides a quantitative assessment of the system's performance, allowing for comparison against baselines. The improvement achieved by RAG clearly highlights its impact.
    * **Clear Architecture:** The paper clearly describes the system architecture and the dual-LLM processing pipeline. The explanation of the task decomposition and API generation stages is well-structured and easy to understand.
    * **Addressing a real-world problem:** The introduction highlights the increasing complexity and sophistication of cyber threats, necessitating adaptive and automated approaches to security enforcement. This clearly demonstrates that the proposed solution provides a compelling answer to that problem.
* **Weaknesses:**

    * **Limited Scope of Evaluation:** The evaluation is limited to attack mitigation policies for Windows systems. It is unclear how well the system would generalize to other operating systems, security tools, or types of security policies (e.g., data loss prevention, access control). There is no discussion as to whether the chosen LLMs and their embeddings might require fine-tuning for other environments.
    * **Dependency on API Documentation:** The system's performance heavily relies on the quality and completeness of the API documentation stored in the vector database. The paper doesn't address potential issues such as outdated, inaccurate, or missing documentation. This creates a single point of failure.
    * **Potential for Misconfiguration:** While the paper aims to reduce human error, there remains a risk of system misconfigurations resulting from inaccurate policy-to-API translation. The paper does not comprehensively address the security implications of those misconfigurations. An adversary might learn to trigger undesirable behaviour on the side of the automated attack mitigation policies, effectively sabotaging the system by leveraging the LLMs' translation capabilities.
    * **Lack of Comparison to Existing Security Automation Tools:** The paper focuses on manual interpretation. It would be important to compare the tool to existing security automation tools.
    * **Limited discussion on prompt engineering:** The success of the LLM driven solution critically relies on the prompt engineering. The paper does not elaborate on prompt selection strategies and optimisation. It would benefit from discussing this.

* **Significance and Potential Influence:**

    * The paper demonstrates the potential of LLMs for automating security policy enforcement, a task that is traditionally labor-intensive and error-prone.
    * The RAG-enhanced approach provides a promising solution for bridging the gap between high-level policy descriptions and low-level executable actions.
    * The framework can be extended to integrate new tools and APIs, supporting scalability and adaptability across diverse security environments.
    * However, the limitations discussed above suggest that further research is needed to address the generalizability, reliability, and security implications of the approach before it can be widely adopted.
* **Novelty**
 The novel application of RAG and dual LLMs to automate security policy enforcement, translating high-level policies into executable API calls, represents a noteworthy advancement. The integration and specific focus on attack mitigation for Windows systems provide a practical contribution, setting this work apart from general applications of LLMs in cybersecurity.

* **Score Justification:**
    While the paper presents a promising approach with solid empirical validation, the limitations in scope, reliance on documentation quality, and potential security implications prevent it from receiving a higher score. However, it provides a valuable contribution to an important area of research and has the potential to influence future work in automated security policy enforcement.

**Score: 6**

- **Classification**: cs.CR
- **Score**: 6/10

### Multiple-Choice Question Generation Using Large Language Models: Methodology and Educator Insights
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.04851v1)
- **Authors**: Giorgio Biancini, Alessio Ferrato, Carla Limongelli
- **Abstract**: Integrating Artificial Intelligence (AI) in educational settings has brought new learning approaches, transforming the practices of both students and educators. Among the various technologies driving this transformation, Large Language Models (LLMs) have emerged as powerful tools for creating educational materials and question answering, but there are still space for new applications. Educators commonly use Multiple-Choice Questions (MCQs) to assess student knowledge, but manually generating these questions is resource-intensive and requires significant time and cognitive effort. In our opinion, LLMs offer a promising solution to these challenges. This paper presents a novel comparative analysis of three widely known LLMs - Llama 2, Mistral, and GPT-3.5 - to explore their potential for creating informative and challenging MCQs. In our approach, we do not rely on the knowledge of the LLM, but we inject the knowledge into the prompt to contrast the hallucinations, giving the educators control over the test's source text, too. Our experiment involving 21 educators shows that GPT-3.5 generates the most effective MCQs across several known metrics. Additionally, it shows that there is still some reluctance to adopt AI in the educational field. This study sheds light on the potential of LLMs to generate MCQs and improve the educational experience, providing valuable insights for the future.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper:

**Summary:**

The paper explores the potential of Large Language Models (LLMs) for generating Multiple-Choice Questions (MCQs), a task traditionally resource-intensive for educators. It presents a comparative analysis of three LLMs (Llama 2, Mistral, and GPT-3.5) using a novel prompting approach that injects knowledge from a provided text into the prompt, aiming to mitigate hallucinations and allow educators control over the source material. An experiment involving 21 educators was conducted to evaluate the quality of generated MCQs based on metrics such as compliance, clarity, distractor selection, coherence, and learning utility. The results suggest that GPT-3.5 outperforms Llama 2 and Mistral in generating effective MCQs. The study also reveals a degree of reluctance among educators towards adopting AI tools in education.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's novelty lies in its specific focus on MCQ generation using LLMs, differentiating it from much of the existing literature that explores LLMs for general question-answering. The authors also emphasize their prompting method that injects external knowledge rather than relying solely on the LLM's pre-trained knowledge, which is a potentially useful approach to address common problems such as hallucinations.

*   **Significance:** The paper addresses a practical problem for educators – the time-consuming creation of effective assessment materials. The research shows promising evidence that LLMs can assist in this task, potentially freeing up educator time for other activities. The user study provides valuable insights into the perceived quality of MCQs generated by different LLMs, contributing to the understanding of their capabilities and limitations in this specific application. The insight into the educators' attitudes towards LLM adoption is also significant.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   Clearly defined research question and methodology.
    *   Rigorous comparative analysis of three widely-used LLMs.
    *   User study involving educators to provide real-world feedback.
    *   Use of established metrics for evaluating the quality of generated MCQs.
    *   Prompt engineering to mitigate some limitations of LLMs.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The sample size of the user study (21 participants) is relatively small, limiting the generalizability of the findings.
    *   The study does not include a baseline comparison with human-generated MCQs or existing MCQ generation methods, making it difficult to assess the true value-add of the LLM-generated questions.
    *   The paper acknowledges limitations regarding the lack of demographic data on the participants and the use of only one domain.
    *   The "Learning utility" metric did not show statistically significant difference across models, raising questions about its effectiveness in this context.
    *   While the prompt is described, it would be helpful to have a detailed analysis of why this specific prompt was chosen and how the parameters within the prompt were optimized.

*   **Potential Influence:** The study could influence future research in the area of AI-assisted education, particularly in the development of LLM-based tools for assessment creation. The findings could encourage educators and developers to explore the potential of LLMs while being mindful of their limitations.

**Justification of Score:**

Given the strengths and weaknesses, the paper represents a reasonably novel and potentially significant contribution to the field, though it's not a groundbreaking one. The focused application, rigorous comparison, and user study are positive aspects. However, the limitations related to sample size, lack of baseline comparison, and the limited scope of the study constrain the overall impact. The research provides a good starting point for further investigations.

### Improving AI-generated music with user-guided training
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.04852v1)
- **Authors**: Vishwa Mohan Singh, Sai Anirudh Aryasomayajula, Ahan Chatterjee, Beste Aydemir, Rifat Mehreen Amin
- **Abstract**: AI music generation has advanced rapidly, with models like diffusion and autoregressive algorithms enabling high-fidelity outputs. These tools can alter styles, mix instruments, or isolate them. Since sound can be visualized as spectrograms, image-generation algorithms can be applied to generate novel music. However, these algorithms are typically trained on fixed datasets, which makes it challenging for them to interpret and respond to user input accurately. This is especially problematic because music is highly subjective and requires a level of personalization that image generation does not provide. In this work, we propose a human-computation approach to gradually improve the performance of these algorithms based on user interactions. The human-computation element involves aggregating and selecting user ratings to use as the loss function for fine-tuning the model. We employ a genetic algorithm that incorporates user feedback to enhance the baseline performance of a model initially trained on a fixed dataset. The effectiveness of this approach is measured by the average increase in user ratings with each iteration. In the pilot test, the first iteration showed an average rating increase of 0.2 compared to the baseline. The second iteration further improved upon this, achieving an additional increase of 0.39 over the first iteration.
- **Summary**: Okay, here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary**

The paper "Improving AI-generated music with user-guided training" proposes a human-computation approach to enhance AI music generation. The core idea is to iteratively refine a stable diffusion model using user feedback. Users are presented with AI-generated music, and their ratings (along with listening time) are used to update the model's training data via a weighted loss function. A genetic algorithm incorporating user feedback is employed to improve upon an initially trained model. The paper presents a pilot study demonstrating that the approach leads to improvements in average user ratings over successive iterations. The system leverages the combination of strengths of humans and computers, taking human input to enhance the generated music and computer models to generate high-fidelity musical segments.

**Critical Evaluation**

*   **Novelty:** The idea of incorporating user feedback into AI music generation is not entirely new. Systems that allow users to interact with and influence generative models have been explored before. However, the specific combination of techniques presented here, specifically leveraging stable diffusion with a human-in-the-loop genetic algorithm and weighted loss function based on both ratings and listening time, appears to be a novel contribution. The details of how user feedback is aggregated and used to refine the model, especially the SLERP and VQ-VAE embeddings with a weighted loss, enhance the significance. The user feedback loop to enhance the learning process is the major point of novelty in the paper.

*   **Significance:** The significance of this work lies in its potential to make AI music generation more personalized and responsive to user preferences. Traditional methods often struggle to capture the subjective nature of music. By iteratively refining the model based on real user feedback, the system can potentially create music that is more tailored to individual tastes. The pilot study showing an increase in user ratings supports this claim. The approach also addresses the limitations of relying solely on fixed datasets, allowing the model to adapt to evolving user preferences. This human-in-the-loop methodology can be generalized to other data generation fields as well.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   The paper clearly outlines the proposed approach and the underlying algorithms.
    *   The pilot study provides empirical evidence supporting the effectiveness of the system, at least in initial iterations.
    *   The paper addresses a key limitation of current AI music generation models: the inability to personalize outputs.
    *   The architecture is comprehensive, showing how the diffusion model can be integrated with human input to make a closed feedback loop.
    *   The use of VQ-VAE, SLERP and DDIM creates a powerful and high-fidelity music generation pipeline that can benefit many industries.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The pilot study involves a small group of users (5 for initial model evaluations and 50 in later stages), which limits the generalizability of the results.
    *   The paper lacks a deeper analysis of the types of musical changes that the system is making based on user feedback.
    *   The approach seems best suited for evolving existing styles rather than creating entirely new musical genres. It will take the average of given music and will be limited to the data that is presented as input.
    *   The long term effectiveness of the system is questionable. Will improvements plateau?
    *   It is unclear how the ratings are weighted and what impact individual bias might have on the end result.

*   **Potential Influence:**
    *   The paper could inspire further research into human-in-the-loop AI music generation systems.
    *   The proposed approach could be adapted to other creative domains where personalization is important.
    *   The paper can serve as a basis for the development of improved methods for aggregating user feedback and incorporating it into generative models.

*   **Justification:** While the paper presents a promising approach, it's important to recognize the limitations of the pilot study and the need for more extensive evaluation. The concepts, while not wholly original, are creatively integrated and applied within the specific context of AI music generation. The iterative approach using user feedback to refine diffusion models is a valuable contribution to the field, despite needing further research. The use of weights to prevent outliers from affecting the outcome is well handled. The integration between the components is well explained and adds to the novelty of the paper.

**Score: 7**

**Rationale:** A score of 7 reflects a reasonably high level of novelty, with a sound methodology and an experimental validation of the concept. The paper addresses a real challenge in AI music generation and offers a creative solution. However, the limited scale of the pilot study and some unanswered questions about the stability and long-term effectiveness prevent a higher score. Further investigation to address the shortcomings would be necessary to place the paper at a 8 or higher.

- **Classification**: cs.SD
- **Score**: 7/10

### Prompting LLMs: Length Control for Isometric Machine Translation
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.04855v1)
- **Authors**: Dávid Javorský, Ondřej Bojar, François Yvon
- **Abstract**: In this study, we explore the effectiveness of isometric machine translation across multiple language pairs (En$\to$De, En$\to$Fr, and En$\to$Es) under the conditions of the IWSLT Isometric Shared Task 2022. Using eight open-source large language models (LLMs) of varying sizes, we investigate how different prompting strategies, varying numbers of few-shot examples, and demonstration selection influence translation quality and length control. We discover that the phrasing of instructions, when aligned with the properties of the provided demonstrations, plays a crucial role in controlling the output length. Our experiments show that LLMs tend to produce shorter translations only when presented with extreme examples, while isometric demonstrations often lead to the models disregarding length constraints. While few-shot prompting generally enhances translation quality, further improvements are marginal across 5, 10, and 20-shot settings. Finally, considering multiple outputs allows to notably improve overall tradeoff between the length and quality, yielding state-of-the-art performance for some language pairs.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper explores the effectiveness of length control in large language models (LLMs) for isometric machine translation across English to German, French, and Spanish. Using a range of open-source LLMs, the authors investigate how different prompting strategies, varying numbers of few-shot examples, and demonstration selection influence translation quality and length compliance.  They find that matching the phrasing of instructions to the properties of the demonstrations is crucial for controlling output length. LLMs tend to produce shorter translations when presented with extreme examples, while isometric demonstrations often hinder length constraint adherence.  Generating multiple outputs with different example sets is shown to improve the trade-off between length and quality.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The novelty of this paper lies primarily in its systematic exploration of various prompting strategies and demonstration selection methods for length control in isometric MT using a range of recently released open-source LLMs. While previous work has investigated prompting and few-shot learning for MT, this paper focuses specifically on the isometric constraint and provides a comprehensive analysis of how different strategies affect length compliance and translation quality *simultaneously*. It addresses a practically important task within machine translation that often requires specialized techniques, especially in scenarios like subtitling and dubbing.

*   **Significance:** The findings have significance for the following reasons:

    *   **Practical Implications:**  The identification of successful prompting strategies offers practical guidance for practitioners working on isometric MT, especially in low-resource settings where fine-tuning is not feasible. The observation that generating multiple outputs can improve the length/quality trade-off is also valuable for practical applications.
    *   **Insights into LLM Behavior:** The study sheds light on how LLMs respond to different types of prompts and demonstrations related to length constraints. For example, the finding that isometric demonstrations can be counterproductive highlights a subtle aspect of LLM behavior that needs careful consideration. The study of prompt matching is also noteworthy, showing the importance of instruction wording that is aligned with demo properties.
    *   **Resource Contribution:** The public release of the collected data is a valuable contribution to the research community, enabling further investigation and comparison of different approaches to isometric MT using LLMs.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Comprehensive Evaluation:** The paper performs a thorough evaluation of different prompting strategies, demonstration selection methods, and LLMs across multiple language pairs.
    *   **Clear Findings:** The paper presents clear and actionable findings about how to improve length control in isometric MT.
    *   **Practical Focus:** The research is motivated by a real-world problem and offers practical solutions that can be easily implemented.
    *   **Reproducibility:** Releasing data and detailed experimental setup contributes to reproducibility.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Limited Scope:** The study focuses primarily on prompting-based approaches and does not explore other techniques for length control, such as fine-tuning or constrained decoding.
    *   **Language Pair Focus:** The experiments are limited to English as the source language, which may not generalize to other language directions. Although this choice reflects the IWSLT 2022 setup, extending experiments is essential.
    *   **Evaluation Metrics:** While BERTScore is a good indicator for assessing text similarity, other metrics that assess conciseness, fluency and content preservation are needed. Also COMET should be justified since it's a non reference based automatic quality metric.
    *   **Models:** All the models are evaluated in quantized versions likely resulting in suboptimal translation scores. It will help to compare against original versions.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper is likely to influence future research on length control in MT using LLMs. The insights about prompting strategies and demonstration selection can be used to develop more effective methods for controlling the length and quality of translations. The public release of the data can also facilitate further investigation and comparison of different approaches.

*   **Score Justification:**

The paper makes a worthwhile contribution by providing a systematic empirical study of prompting strategies for isometric machine translation using recent LLMs. It provides useful insights, actionable findings, and a valuable resource for the community. However, the limitations in scope (focusing on English as the source language only and prompting only), evaluation (limited use of conciseness related metrics) and model limitations impact its significance. The paper builds on existing work in prompting for MT, but the focus on the isometric constraint and comprehensive evaluation across various LLMs justify a score above average.

**Score: 7**

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### Sparse Autoencoders, Again?
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.04859v1)
- **Authors**: Yin Lu, Tong He, Xuening Zhu, David Wipf
- **Abstract**: Is there really much more to say about sparse autoencoders (SAEs)? Autoencoders in general, and SAEs in particular, represent deep architectures that are capable of modeling low-dimensional latent structure in data. Such structure could reflect, among other things, correlation patterns in large language model activations, or complex natural image manifolds. And yet despite the wide-ranging applicability, there have been relatively few changes to SAEs beyond the original recipe from decades ago, namely, standard deep encoder/decoder layers trained with a classical/deterministic sparse regularizer applied within the latent space. One possible exception is the variational autoencoder (VAE), which adopts a stochastic encoder module capable of producing sparse representations when applied to manifold data. In this work we formalize underappreciated weaknesses with both canonical SAEs, as well as analogous VAEs applied to similar tasks, and propose a hybrid alternative model that circumvents these prior limitations. In terms of theoretical support, we prove that global minima of our proposed model recover certain forms of structured data spread across a union of manifolds. Meanwhile, empirical evaluations on synthetic and real-world datasets substantiate the efficacy of our approach in accurately estimating underlying manifold dimensions and producing sparser latent representations without compromising reconstruction error. In general, we are able to exceed the performance of equivalent-capacity SAEs and VAEs, as well as recent diffusion models where applicable, within domains such as images and language model activation patterns.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "Sparse Autoencoders, Again?"

**Summary:**

The paper revisits the topic of sparse autoencoders (SAEs), arguing that despite their long history, there's still room for improvement. It identifies limitations in both traditional SAEs and variational autoencoders (VAEs) regarding hyperparameter sensitivity (SAEs) and the inability to produce truly adaptive, sample-specific sparsity patterns (VAEs).  The authors propose a hybrid model called VAEase, a VAE variant that retools the encoder variance to act as an adaptive sparsity selector. They provide theoretical support, demonstrating that VAEase can recover manifold structure, maintain fewer local minima, and empirically show its superiority over SAEs and VAEs in manifold dimension estimation and sparse representation learning, particularly within the context of large language model (LLM) activations.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:**

*   **Incremental Novelty:** The paper's novelty is incremental rather than groundbreaking.  While the core idea of re-purposing VAE encoder variance is clever, it's built upon well-established foundations. The theoretical analysis, while rigorous, doesn't introduce radically new theoretical tools or insights.
*   **VAE Connection:** The connection to VAEs offers a way to alleviate sensitivity towards hyperparameter tunings that plague standard deterministic sparse autoencoders.
*   **Adaptive Sparsity:**  The key advantage claimed, adaptive sparsity in a VAE-like framework, is convincingly demonstrated.  The explicit focus on the adaptive sparsity problem with a hybrid approach, and its theoretical guarantee in recovering union of manifolds, elevates the work beyond a simple architectural tweak.

**Significance:**

*   **Practical Relevance:** The findings have practical significance, especially given the current interest in sparse representations for understanding and interpreting large models. Demonstrating a method that improves upon existing approaches, particularly in LLM activation analysis, is valuable.
*   **Theoretical Justification:** The paper provides a theoretical justification for the proposed approach and performs empirical validation against baselines. This strengthens the claims and provides a deeper understanding of the method's behavior. The theoretical contributions, specifically the analysis of local minima, adds weight to the novelty claim and helps ground an understanding for the advantages of the proposed approach.
*   **LLM Application:** The application to LLM activations is timely, given the ongoing research into interpreting and manipulating large language models. Showing improvement in this area lends immediate relevance to the work.

**Strengths:**

*   **Well-defined Problem:** The paper clearly articulates the shortcomings of existing methods.
*   **Rigorous Analysis:** The theoretical arguments are well-structured and provide a solid foundation for the approach.
*   **Empirical Validation:**  The experimental results are compelling, showcasing VAEase's advantages over SAEs and VAEs on both synthetic and real-world datasets.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Limited Generalizability?**  While the LLM experiments are important, the paper could benefit from testing VAEase on a broader range of applications where sparse autoencoders are commonly used (e.g., denoising, anomaly detection). It would be critical to establish whether a VAE-based approach is necessary in settings that don't require adaptive sparsity.
*   **Computational Cost:** While not explicitly stated, it could be beneficial to establish whether or not VAEase has computational speedups in inference.
*   **Limited Baseline Comparisons:** Comparisons with more recent advances in sparse representation learning (beyond traditional SAEs and VAEs) could further strengthen the paper.

**Potential Influence:**

The paper is likely to influence researchers working on sparse representation learning, particularly those interested in applying these techniques to understand or manipulate LLMs. The VAEase architecture provides a viable alternative to traditional SAEs, offering a combination of benefits that could lead to more effective and interpretable representations. The emphasis on manifold recovery and adaptive sparsity makes it particularly relevant for tasks involving complex, structured data.

**Overall:**

The paper presents a useful and well-supported improvement to the sparse autoencoding landscape. The hybrid approach intelligently leverages the strengths of both VAE and SAE models while addressing their individual limitations. While the novelty isn't transformative, the clear articulation of the problem, the rigorous analysis, and the compelling empirical results make it a valuable contribution.

**Score: 7**

**Rationale:** While a clever extension and with solid theoretical grounding, the paper has a limited scope of comparison, a computational analysis, and potential generalizability concerns. However, the timely application and insightful blend of VAEs and SAEs, as well as the local minima analysis, support the score of 7.

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 7/10

### LLMs for sensory-motor control: Combining in-context and iterative learning
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.04867v1)
- **Authors**: Jônata Tyska Carvalho, Stefano Nolfi
- **Abstract**: We propose a method that enables large language models (LLMs) to control embodied agents by directly mapping continuous observation vectors to continuous action vectors. Initially, the LLMs generate a control strategy based on a textual description of the agent, its environment, and the intended goal. This strategy is then iteratively refined through a learning process in which the LLMs are repeatedly prompted to improve the current strategy, using performance feedback and sensory-motor data collected during its evaluation. The method is validated on classic control tasks from the Gymnasium library and the inverted pendulum task from the MuJoCo library. In most cases, it successfully identifies optimal or high-performing solutions by integrating symbolic knowledge derived through reasoning with sub-symbolic sensory-motor data gathered as the agent interacts with its environment.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper proposes a method for controlling embodied agents using Large Language Models (LLMs). Instead of relying on predefined motor primitives or human demonstrations, the LLM directly generates continuous action vectors from continuous observation vectors, controlling the agent's actuators. The LLM initially generates a control strategy from a textual description of the agent, environment, and goal. This strategy is iteratively refined through in-context learning, where the LLM is prompted to improve its strategy using performance feedback and sensory-motor data collected during evaluation. The method is validated on classic control tasks from the Gymnasium and MuJoCo libraries. The authors demonstrate the capability of their approach to achieve optimal or near-optimal performance through iterative prompting, combining symbolic knowledge with sub-symbolic sensory-motor data.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:**  The paper's novelty lies in its specific combination of existing techniques. While using LLMs for embodied control and iterative prompting have been explored separately, the authors present a structured prompting technique with a particular emphasis on eliminating the need for pre-defined motor primitives *and* human demonstrations, creating a purely autonomous LLM-driven agent learning process.  The prompt engineering design (reflective analysis -> rules -> code) is also a novel element.  Furthermore, using modified Gym environments (Cartpole*1 and Cartpole*2) to prevent the LLM from simply regurgitating memorized solutions demonstrates a genuine attempt to ensure the generated solutions are original and not just memorized patterns. The analysis of successful and unsuccessful behaviors based on sensory-motor data within the prompt loop to promote autonomous iterative learning is a valuable contribution.

**Significance:** The potential significance of this work is considerable. A key promise of LLMs in robotics is the ability to create controllers without the need for extensive task-specific training data or hand-engineered modules.  The paper addresses the "embodiment problem" by showing how LLMs can be directly integrated with sensory data, offering a practical approach to bridge the gap between symbolic reasoning and continuous control. The fact that the method achieves high performance in several benchmark tasks reinforces this claim. Furthermore, showing the iterative learning improves performance and overcomes initial limitations illustrates a crucial advantage. The identification of sub-sampling sensory-motor data as a major bottleneck is a useful pointer for future research.

**Strengths:**

*   **Clear Problem Definition:** The paper clearly articulates the challenges of embodied control and the limitations of existing approaches.
*   **Well-Defined Method:** The proposed prompting technique and iterative learning process are well-defined and easy to understand. The step-by-step prompt generation is a sensible approach.
*   **Empirical Validation:** The method is validated on a range of classic control tasks with statistically significant results.
*   **Original Solution Generation:** The modification of the CartPole environments and analysis of generated solutions provide compelling evidence that the model isn't simply recalling memorized training data.
*   **Emphasis on Autonomous Learning:** This approach does not require human demonstrators or pre-programmed control primitives, which represents a distinct advantage over other approaches.
*   **Comprehensive data access:** The paper provides access to logs, control rules, and videos, offering valuable assets for others to build on their work.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Limited Generalization:** While the paper shows good performance in classic control tasks, the tasks themselves are relatively simple. The real test will be performance in more complex, real-world scenarios.
*   **Computational Cost:** The paper relies on powerful LLMs and significant computational resources, which might limit its accessibility and scalability. Running this on commodity hardware would be challenging.
*   **Pendulum Failure:** The poor performance on the Pendulum task, and the lack of a detailed explanation for this failure is a weakness.  The discussion points to limitations in capturing failure points and iterative learning. This limitation needs to be more explicitly addressed and explored for generalization.
*   **Opaque reasoning:** While the paper highlights the model's iterative learning, the *reasoning* behind why certain modifications were made and why they were effective is not always clear.  More insight into the LLM's decision-making process could strengthen the analysis.
*   **Limited prompt engineering discussion:** Although the prompts are described in the Appendix, a deeper exploration into alternative prompt structures and content is missing. Could the performance be further improved with better prompts?
*   **Reliance on Fixed Base Prompts:** The prompts are manually constructed and are not automatically generated. This limits the generalizability of the method.
*   **Data sub-sampling:** Only including 20 steps from each evaluation can hinder learning, particularly in complex tasks. The paper addresses this.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper makes a solid contribution by demonstrating a prompting method that empowers LLMs to autonomously control embodied agents in classic control tasks without relying on either human demonstrations or pre-defined motor primitives. While the limitations in generalizing to complex real-world scenarios, heavy computational demands, the Pendulum failure, reasoning opacity and the current reliance on manual prompts are critical points, the work showcases the potential of LLMs for a specific task and provides a valuable starting point for future research. It presents a structured approach to iterative learning, which provides a framework to improve the model's control strategy based on sensory-motor feedback. Although the core components are not entirely novel, the combined approach and demonstration of autonomous learning warrant a score that reflects a noteworthy, yet not groundbreaking, contribution.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.AI
- **Score**: 7/10

### Invisible Backdoor Triggers in Image Editing Model via Deep Watermarking
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.04879v1)
- **Authors**: Yu-Feng Chen, Tzuhsuan Huang, Pin-Yen Chiu, Jun-Cheng Chen
- **Abstract**: Diffusion models have achieved remarkable progress in both image generation and editing. However, recent studies have revealed their vulnerability to backdoor attacks, in which specific patterns embedded in the input can manipulate the model's behavior. Most existing research in this area has proposed attack frameworks focused on the image generation pipeline, leaving backdoor attacks in image editing relatively unexplored. Among the few studies targeting image editing, most utilize visible triggers, which are impractical because they introduce noticeable alterations to the input image before editing. In this paper, we propose a novel attack framework that embeds invisible triggers into the image editing process via poisoned training data. We leverage off-the-shelf deep watermarking models to encode imperceptible watermarks as backdoor triggers. Our goal is to make the model produce the predefined backdoor target when it receives watermarked inputs, while editing clean images normally according to the given prompt. With extensive experiments across different watermarking models, the proposed method achieves promising attack success rates. In addition, the analysis results of the watermark characteristics in term of backdoor attack further support the effectiveness of our approach. The code is available at:https://github.com/aiiu-lab/BackdoorImageEditing
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces a novel backdoor attack framework for image editing diffusion models. Instead of relying on visible triggers, it uses off-the-shelf deep watermarking models to embed imperceptible triggers into the training data. During inference, if an input image contains the embedded watermark, the model generates a predefined backdoor target. The approach aims to maintain visual integrity while providing security against unauthorized edits. The authors conduct experiments with various watermarking models and analyze the watermark characteristics that lead to successful backdoor attacks. They demonstrate robustness against distortions like erasing and JPEG compression.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper presents a relatively novel approach by leveraging deep watermarking for backdoor attacks in image editing diffusion models. While backdoor attacks and watermarking are established areas, their combination for this specific application, particularly focusing on *invisible* triggers, distinguishes this work from existing research. Compared to prior work utilizing visible triggers, this approach represents a significant advancement in stealth.

*   **Significance:** The paper's significance lies in addressing a practical vulnerability in image editing models. As diffusion models become more widespread, ensuring their security against malicious manipulation is crucial. The ability to inject hidden triggers that can force the model to generate specific, unintended outputs poses a real threat. The framework presented offers a method to analyze and potentially defend against such attacks. The focus on maintaining visual integrity is also important, as it addresses a limitation of existing backdoor methods.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   The use of *invisible* watermarks is a strong point, making the attack more practical and difficult to detect.
    *   The experimental results demonstrate promising attack success rates with different watermarking models.
    *   The analysis of watermark properties provides valuable insights into the factors influencing the attack's effectiveness.
    *   The evaluation of robustness against various image distortions highlights the limitations and strengths of the approach in real-world scenarios.
    *   Clear problem statement and motivation.
    *   Detailed methodology and experimental setup.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The paper mainly focuses on demonstrating the attack's feasibility. A more in-depth discussion on potential *defenses* against this type of attack would significantly increase its impact.  While the paper is strong on the *attack* side, it is significantly weaker on the *defense* side.
    *   The performance under certain distortions (rotation, blur, etc.) is still limited, suggesting areas for improvement.
    *   While the quantitative values for watermarked image quality are presented, more extensive perceptual studies or user evaluations could strengthen the claim that the watermarks are indeed imperceptible.
    *   The paper relies heavily on existing watermarking models; more innovation in the watermarking component itself would enhance the contribution.
    *   Limited discussion about scaling this to various backdoor targets

*   **Potential Impact:** The paper has the potential to influence research in backdoor attacks, watermarking, and security of diffusion models. It could inspire further work on developing more robust backdoor attacks or, conversely, defense mechanisms against such attacks.  The analysis of latent space manipulation is also valuable for understanding the vulnerabilities of diffusion models.

*   **Critical Remarks:** While the paper is well-written and technically sound, the overall innovation, while present, is somewhat incremental. It uses established watermarking techniques and integrates them into a backdoor attack framework. The true significance will depend on whether this research sparks the development of practical countermeasures.

**Score: 6.5**

**Justification:**

The score reflects the paper's novelty and significance being in the *application* of existing watermarking methods to a relevant problem in diffusion model security. The relatively straightforward adaptation of existing watermarking techniques means there is some novelty, but it is less than if the authors had introduced a novel watermarking method designed to be exceptionally effective at backdoor injection. A higher score would be warranted if the paper had addressed the defense implications of the attack or provided a deeper theoretical understanding of how diffusion models are vulnerable to this type of manipulation. Also, there's limited discussion in terms of scalability with various backdoor targets. Thus, a score of 6.5 reflects a good contribution with potential for further research, but not a groundbreaking advancement in the field. The strength lies in its practicality and clear demonstration, but the lack of focus on defenses is a key limitation.

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 6/10

### Evaluating the Effectiveness of Linguistic Knowledge in Pretrained Language Models: A Case Study of Universal Dependencies
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.04887v1)
- **Authors**: Wenxi Li
- **Abstract**: Universal Dependencies (UD), while widely regarded as the most successful linguistic framework for cross-lingual syntactic representation, remains underexplored in terms of its effectiveness. This paper addresses this gap by integrating UD into pretrained language models and assesses if UD can improve their performance on a cross-lingual adversarial paraphrase identification task. Experimental results show that incorporation of UD yields significant improvements in accuracy and $F_1$ scores, with average gains of 3.85\% and 6.08\% respectively. These enhancements reduce the performance gap between pretrained models and large language models in some language pairs, and even outperform the latter in some others. Furthermore, the UD-based similarity score between a given language and English is positively correlated to the performance of models in that language. Both findings highlight the validity and potential of UD in out-of-domain tasks.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

This paper investigates the effectiveness of Universal Dependencies (UD) in enhancing the performance of pretrained language models (PLMs) on a cross-lingual adversarial paraphrase identification (PI) task. The authors integrate UD syntactic information into PLMs by converting UD dependency structures into hypergraphs and constructing a similarity matrix based on lexical alignment and label comparison. This similarity matrix is then injected into the attention mechanism of PLMs. The experiments on the PAWS-X dataset show that incorporating UD information improves the accuracy and F1 scores of PLMs, making them more competitive with large language models (LLMs). Furthermore, the paper finds a positive correlation between UD-based similarity scores and model performance, suggesting that UD captures syntactic distances between languages. The authors conclude that UD can improve PLMs' cross-lingual generalization capabilities.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Addresses an important gap:** The paper tackles a relative lack of research exploring the effectiveness of UD in tasks beyond parsing and annotation.
    *   **Novel approach:** The method of converting UD structures into hypergraphs and integrating a similarity matrix into PLM attention is innovative.  The use of hypergraphs is particularly interesting as it allows capturing of more complex syntactic relations than simple trees.
    *   **Empirical validation:** The experimental results show a consistent improvement across different PLMs with the integration of UD information. The results on the PAWS-X dataset is well-conducted.
    *   **Interesting findings:** The correlation between UD-based similarity scores and model performance provides valuable insight into how UD captures cross-lingual syntactic relationships.
    *   **Comparison with LLMs:** The inclusion of a LLM as a reference highlights the potential of UD to bridge the performance gap between PLMs and LLMs in cross-lingual tasks.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Limited LLM evaluation:** Using just one LLM as a reference is insufficient to make strong claims about closing the gap. LLMs vary greatly in their performance, and a more thorough evaluation with multiple LLMs is warranted.  Furthermore, the paper only fine-tunes the linear layers of the LLM, which might not be sufficient to fully realize its capabilities.
    *   **Task specificity:** The cross-lingual adversarial PI task, while a good testbed, is somewhat specific. The extent to which the findings generalize to other cross-lingual tasks is unclear. More diverse tasks would strengthen the arguments.
    *   **Lack of ablation studies:** The paper lacks ablation studies to understand the contribution of each component of the UD integration method. For instance, how much does the hypergraph transformation contribute versus simply injecting the dependency labels? More fine-grained analyses are needed to optimize the method.
    *   **Oversimplification of syntactic similarity:** The average similarity score is a very coarse measure of syntactic relatedness.  More nuanced syntactic metrics could be considered, potentially providing richer insights.

*   **Novelty:**

    The paper presents a novel way of incorporating UD information into PLMs.  The use of hypergraphs and similarity matrices for cross-lingual syntactic representation is unique, and the positive results demonstrate the potential of this approach. While dependency kernels have been explored before, this paper takes a more direct approach by injecting syntactic similarity into the attention mechanism.

*   **Significance:**

    The paper has the potential to influence research in both UD and cross-lingual NLP. It provides an example of how UD can be used to improve the performance of PLMs on cross-lingual tasks. The finding that UD-based similarity scores are correlated with model performance could lead to a better understanding of cross-lingual transfer learning. However, the results are limited due to using only one task, and the analysis has some weaknesses.

**Justification of Score:**

While the paper has several strengths, including a novel approach, empirical validation, and interesting findings, its weaknesses limit its overall impact. The LLM evaluation is insufficient, task specificity is a concern, ablation studies are lacking, and some analysis can be made deeper. The presented improvements, albeit present, are moderate. It would be interesting, for instance, to compare the UD attention mechanism with other methods, for instance adding UD info to the input embeddings. The limitations prevent it from being a highly influential paper. The potential to close the gap between LLMs and PLMs is a great avenue, but more work is necessary.

Score: 6

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 6/10

### ICPC-Eval: Probing the Frontiers of LLM Reasoning with Competitive Programming Contests
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.04894v1)
- **Authors**: Shiyi Xu, Yiwen Hu, Yingqian Min, Zhipeng Chen, Wayne Xin Zhao, Ji-Rong Wen
- **Abstract**: With the significant progress of large reasoning models in complex coding and reasoning tasks, existing benchmarks, like LiveCodeBench and CodeElo, are insufficient to evaluate the coding capabilities of large language models (LLMs) in real competition environments. Moreover, current evaluation metrics such as Pass@K fail to capture the reflective abilities of reasoning models. To address these challenges, we propose \textbf{ICPC-Eval}, a top-level competitive coding benchmark designed to probing the frontiers of LLM reasoning. ICPC-Eval includes 118 carefully curated problems from 11 recent ICPC contests held in various regions of the world, offering three key contributions: 1) A challenging realistic ICPC competition scenario, featuring a problem type and difficulty distribution consistent with actual contests. 2) A robust test case generation method and a corresponding local evaluation toolkit, enabling efficient and accurate local evaluation. 3) An effective test-time scaling evaluation metric, Refine@K, which allows iterative repair of solutions based on execution feedback. The results underscore the significant challenge in evaluating complex reasoning abilities: top-tier reasoning models like DeepSeek-R1 often rely on multi-turn code feedback to fully unlock their in-context reasoning potential when compared to non-reasoning counterparts. Furthermore, despite recent advancements in code generation, these models still lag behind top-performing human teams. We release the benchmark at: https://github.com/RUCAIBox/Slow_Thinking_with_LLMs
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces ICPC-Eval, a new benchmark designed to evaluate the reasoning capabilities of large language models (LLMs) in the context of competitive programming. Addressing the limitations of existing benchmarks (insufficient difficulty and unrealistic evaluation methodologies), ICPC-Eval features 118 problems curated from recent ICPC contests. Key contributions include: (1) a challenging, realistic ICPC competition scenario, (2) a robust test case generation method with a local evaluation toolkit, and (3) an effective test-time scaling evaluation metric called Refine@K, which allows for iterative repair of solutions based on execution feedback. The authors evaluate 15 LLMs, finding that even top-tier models struggle to match human performance in ICPC competitions.  They also demonstrate that Refine@K provides a more nuanced evaluation of reasoning abilities compared to the traditional Pass@K metric. The benchmark and evaluation toolkit are publicly released.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Strengths:**
    * **Addressing a Real Gap:** The paper clearly identifies and addresses a significant gap in existing LLM evaluation benchmarks. As LLMs become more sophisticated, simple coding challenges become insufficient for assessing true reasoning capabilities. ICPC-Eval fills this void by offering problems that require complex algorithmic thinking and problem-solving skills representative of real-world competitive programming.
    * **Realistic Evaluation:** The Refine@K metric is a valuable contribution.  It acknowledges the iterative nature of problem-solving, especially in challenging contexts like ICPC. Allowing models to refine their solutions based on feedback is a more realistic simulation of human problem-solving than simply generating multiple independent samples.
    * **Local Evaluation Toolkit:** The provision of a local evaluation toolkit is important for accessibility and reproducibility.  The methodology for generating test cases is well described, including the use of LLMs to create both random and edge-case inputs. This reduces reliance on external online judges, which can be unreliable and have undisclosed test sets.
    * **Comprehensive Evaluation:** The paper includes a comprehensive evaluation of a range of state-of-the-art LLMs, providing valuable insights into their strengths and weaknesses in the context of competitive programming. The comparative analysis with other benchmarks and the ablation study comparing Refine@K and Pass@K further strengthen the findings.

* **Weaknesses:**
    * **LLM Dependence for Test Case Generation:** While using LLMs to generate test cases is innovative, it also introduces a potential source of bias and uncertainty. The quality and diversity of the test cases depend heavily on the capabilities of the LLM used for generation. There's a risk that the generated test cases might not be truly representative of all possible inputs, especially for complex algorithmic problems. The validation process is crucial but also relies on human judgement to some degree.
    * **Limited Number of Contests:** Although the authors state they plan to expand in the future, the current dataset is drawn from only 11 recent contests. A larger, more diverse set of problems could further enhance the benchmark's representativeness and robustness.
    * **Practicality Concerns:** Using the test-time scaling via refine approach could be prohibitively expensive in production, especially if the inference needs to be done from scratch in each turn.

* **Novelty and Significance:**
    * The *combination* of challenging problems, a realistic evaluation setting (Refine@K), and a local evaluation toolkit is genuinely novel. Existing benchmarks tend to focus on either problem difficulty or evaluation methodology, but ICPC-Eval addresses both.
    * The benchmark is highly *significant* to the LLM research community, as it provides a new tool for evaluating and pushing the boundaries of LLM reasoning capabilities. The focus on competitive programming aligns with the growing interest in using LLMs to tackle complex, real-world problems.
    * The Refine@K metric could influence how LLMs are evaluated in other tasks beyond coding, particularly those that involve iterative refinement and feedback.

* **Influence on the Field:**
    * ICPC-Eval is likely to become a widely adopted benchmark for evaluating LLMs in the context of competitive programming. The public release of the dataset and evaluation toolkit will facilitate further research and development in this area.
    * The Refine@K metric could inspire new evaluation methodologies that better capture the iterative nature of problem-solving.
    * The benchmark could also drive the development of new LLM architectures and training techniques specifically designed to excel in competitive programming and other complex reasoning tasks.

**Overall:**
The paper presents a strong contribution to the field of LLM evaluation. While there are some limitations related to the dependence on LLMs for test case generation and the size of the dataset, the overall novelty and significance of the work are substantial. ICPC-Eval provides a valuable new tool for researchers and practitioners interested in pushing the boundaries of LLM reasoning capabilities. The paper is well-written, the methodology is clearly described, and the results are thoroughly analyzed.  The combination of a challenging benchmark and a novel evaluation metric makes this paper a significant step forward in the field.
