### Transformers Meet In-Context Learning: A Universal Approximation Theory
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.05200v1)
- **Authors**: Gen Li, Yuchen Jiao, Yu Huang, Yuting Wei, Yuxin Chen
- **Abstract**: Modern large language models are capable of in-context learning, the ability to perform new tasks at inference time using only a handful of input-output examples in the prompt, without any fine-tuning or parameter updates. We develop a universal approximation theory to better understand how transformers enable in-context learning. For any class of functions (each representing a distinct task), we demonstrate how to construct a transformer that, without any further weight updates, can perform reliable prediction given only a few in-context examples. In contrast to much of the recent literature that frames transformers as algorithm approximators -- i.e., constructing transformers to emulate the iterations of optimization algorithms as a means to approximate solutions of learning problems -- our work adopts a fundamentally different approach rooted in universal function approximation. This alternative approach offers approximation guarantees that are not constrained by the effectiveness of the optimization algorithms being approximated, thereby extending far beyond convex problems and linear function classes. Our construction sheds light on how transformers can simultaneously learn general-purpose representations and adapt dynamically to in-context examples.
- **Summary**: Okay, I've analyzed the provided text. Here's a summary, critical evaluation, and score for the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper "Transformers Meet In-Context Learning: A Universal Approximation Theory" investigates the ability of transformers to perform in-context learning (ICL) from a universal approximation perspective. Unlike much of the existing literature that frames transformers as algorithm approximators (emulating optimization algorithms), this work develops a theory demonstrating how transformers can directly approximate functions from a general class *F* without weight updates. The authors provide approximation guarantees for a transformer that, given a few in-context examples, can reliably predict outputs for any function within *F*.  The prediction error is bounded, demonstrating a logarithmic dependence on the covering number of the function class, indicating that the transformer's complexity needs only grow logarithmically with the complexity of the function class being learned. They focus on demonstrating existence and achievability rather than specific training procedures.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The key novelty lies in taking a universal function approximation approach to in-context learning, contrasting with the prevalent view of transformers as algorithm approximators. Showing that transformers can *directly* represent a function class for ICL is a significant departure. Showing approximation guarantees for a general class of functions (beyond linear and convex cases addressed in prior algorithm approximation work) is also a significant contribution. Showing that a transformer can *simultaneously* learn many functions in the function class is also novel.

*   **Significance:** The significance is multifaceted:

    *   **Theoretical Understanding of ICL:** This work provides a different and arguably more fundamental theoretical perspective on how transformers achieve ICL.  It moves beyond the limitations imposed by the convergence properties of the optimization algorithms being approximated in other approaches.
    *   **Design Implications:** The constructive proof, while not immediately providing a practical training procedure, offers insights into the architectural choices (depth, input dimension) needed for effective ICL in transformers.
    *   **Broader Applicability:**  By addressing more general function classes, this work expands the theoretical applicability of ICL analysis, offering a framework relevant to a wider range of real-world tasks.
    *   **Beyond Algorithm Approximation:** It pushes beyond the idea that Transformers are simply doing algorithm approximation, potentially motivating a search for other key components enabling their ICL success.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Clear Problem Formulation:** The paper precisely defines the in-context learning setting and the goals of universal approximation.
    *   **Rigorous Theoretical Analysis:** The proofs, while complex, appear mathematically sound, providing strong guarantees.
    *   **Novel Perspective:** The shift in perspective from algorithm approximation to direct function approximation is compelling and valuable.
    *   **General Function Classes:** The analysis extends beyond simple settings like linear regression.
    *   **Constructive Proof:** Proves existence and shows *how* such a transformer can be constructed.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Practicality:** The paper is highly theoretical. It proves the *existence* of a transformer with the stated properties but does not offer a directly implementable or scalable training algorithm. The analysis is asymptotic, and some constants in the bounds might be large in practice.
    *   **Complexity:** The specific architectural details derived from the constructive proof could be complex and not necessarily optimal in practice.
    *   **Logarithmic Dependence on Covering Number:** While a logarithmic dependency is theoretically appealing, for function classes with a very large covering number, even this logarithmic scaling may pose a challenge in practice.
    *   **Focus on Approximation:** The paper deals with *approximation*, not with learning dynamics, so it is not immediately clear what the trade offs are as it relates to pre-training large transformers.
    *   The main theorem makes a statement "with probability at least 1 - O(N^-10)". While it is high probability, it would have been nice if this result held for every f in F with a deterministic bound.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper's theoretical insights could inspire new architectural designs or training strategies for transformers, leading to more effective ICL in practice. It also opens avenues for further theoretical research on the fundamental limits of ICL and the representation power of transformers.

**Justification of Score:**

The paper presents a significant and novel theoretical contribution to the understanding of in-context learning. While the lack of immediate practicality is a limitation, the conceptual shift and rigorous analysis provide a valuable foundation for future research.  The universal approximation perspective is a powerful lens through which to analyze transformers. The results concerning the complexity of the transformer's architecture with respect to the complexity of the function class are quite insightful. However, the highly theoretical nature of the paper, combined with the lack of empirical validation and algorithmic details, prevents it from receiving a very high score.

### OGGSplat: Open Gaussian Growing for Generalizable Reconstruction with Expanded Field-of-View
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.05204v1)
- **Authors**: Yanbo Wang, Ziyi Wang, Wenzhao Zheng, Jie Zhou, Jiwen Lu
- **Abstract**: Reconstructing semantic-aware 3D scenes from sparse views is a challenging yet essential research direction, driven by the demands of emerging applications such as virtual reality and embodied AI. Existing per-scene optimization methods require dense input views and incur high computational costs, while generalizable approaches often struggle to reconstruct regions outside the input view cone. In this paper, we propose OGGSplat, an open Gaussian growing method that expands the field-of-view in generalizable 3D reconstruction. Our key insight is that the semantic attributes of open Gaussians provide strong priors for image extrapolation, enabling both semantic consistency and visual plausibility. Specifically, once open Gaussians are initialized from sparse views, we introduce an RGB-semantic consistent inpainting module applied to selected rendered views. This module enforces bidirectional control between an image diffusion model and a semantic diffusion model. The inpainted regions are then lifted back into 3D space for efficient and progressive Gaussian parameter optimization. To evaluate our method, we establish a Gaussian Outpainting (GO) benchmark that assesses both semantic and generative quality of reconstructed open-vocabulary scenes. OGGSplat also demonstrates promising semantic-aware scene reconstruction capabilities when provided with two view images captured directly from a smartphone camera.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper "OGGSplat: Open Gaussian Growing for Generalizable Reconstruction with Expanded Field-of-View" addresses the challenge of reconstructing 3D scenes from sparse views, with a focus on expanding the field-of-view (FOV) beyond the input images while maintaining semantic consistency.  The key idea is to leverage the semantic information embedded in open Gaussians as a prior for image extrapolation. The proposed method, OGGSplat, initializes Gaussians from sparse views and then iteratively expands them using an RGB-semantic consistent inpainting module. This module enforces bidirectional control between an image diffusion model and a semantic diffusion model, ensuring that inpainted regions are both visually plausible and semantically coherent.  A new benchmark, Gaussian Outpainting (GO), is introduced to evaluate the approach, assessing both semantic and generative quality in extrapolated regions. Experiments demonstrate that OGGSplat outperforms existing methods in generating consistent and plausible 3D scene reconstructions from sparse inputs with expanded FOV.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Strengths:**

*   **Addresses a relevant problem:** Reconstructing 3D scenes from sparse views, especially with expanded FOV and semantic awareness, is a significant challenge with practical applications in robotics, VR/AR, and embodied AI.
*   **Novel Approach:**  The RGB-semantic consistent inpainting module and the progressive Gaussian growing strategy appear to be novel. The bidirectional control between image and semantic diffusion models is a particularly interesting aspect.
*   **New Benchmark:** The Gaussian Outpainting (GO) benchmark is a valuable contribution, providing a standardized way to evaluate the quality of extrapolated 3D scene reconstructions in terms of both visual fidelity and semantic plausibility.  The use of multiple open-vocabulary segmentation models for ground truth generation is a good approach.
*   **Strong experimental results:** The quantitative and qualitative results demonstrate that OGGSplat outperforms existing methods in terms of both visual quality (FID) and semantic accuracy (mIoU). The results on real smartphone captured images are also compelling.
*   **Clear presentation:** The paper is well-written and clearly explains the proposed method and experimental setup. The architecture overview is helpful.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Reliance on Diffusion Models:** While the use of diffusion models is a strength, it also introduces potential limitations in terms of computational cost and dependence on the quality of pre-trained models. More detailed complexity analysis, with specific time breakdowns would be helpful.
*   **Limited to Indoor Scenes:** The paper acknowledges that the current implementation is primarily suited for indoor scenes due to challenges in depth estimation in outdoor environments. This limits the generalizability of the approach. Depth estimation is based on custom-trained monocular networks, rather than learning depth as part of the overall training process, or relying on pretrained networks. The advantages/disadvantages of this design choice should be clarified.
*   **Incremental Improvement?** The paper shows good results, but it would be stronger if it provided insight into how the contributions interact. Does each component add to the overall improvements, or is performance mostly driven by one component?
*   **Benchmark Limitations**: As mentioned in the paper, FID scores remain high, indicating challenges in full visual realism. The benchmark also constrains the available data and might not fully capture the complexity of diverse real-world scenes.

**Novelty and Significance:**

The paper presents a genuinely novel approach to expanding the field-of-view in generalizable 3D scene reconstruction. The RGB-semantic consistent inpainting module is a clever way to leverage semantic information to guide image extrapolation and maintain consistency. The introduction of the GO benchmark is also a significant contribution, as it provides a much-needed tool for evaluating the quality of extrapolated 3D scenes. While the reliance on diffusion models and the limitation to indoor scenes are drawbacks, the overall approach shows promise and has the potential to influence future research in this area.

**Potential Influence:**

The OGGSplat method and the GO benchmark have the potential to become important tools for researchers working on 3D scene reconstruction, view synthesis, and embodied AI. The focus on semantic consistency in extrapolated regions is particularly relevant for applications where scene understanding is crucial.

**Score:**

Score: 7

**Justification:**

The paper demonstrates a novel and effective approach to expanding the field-of-view in 3D scene reconstruction while maintaining semantic consistency. The introduction of the GO benchmark is a valuable contribution. The limitations related to reliance on diffusion models and applicability to indoor scenes somewhat temper the impact, but the overall contribution is significant. Further improvements in depth estimation and exploration of alternative generative models could further enhance the method's performance and generalizability.

### RELIC: Evaluating Compositional Instruction Following via Language Recognition
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.05205v1)
- **Authors**: Jackson Petty, Michael Y. Hu, Wentao Wang, Shauli Ravfogel, William Merrill, Tal Linzen
- **Abstract**: Large language models (LLMs) are increasingly expected to perform tasks based only on a specification of the task provided in context, without examples of inputs and outputs; this ability is referred to as instruction following. We introduce the Recognition of Languages In-Context (RELIC) framework to evaluate instruction following using language recognition: the task of determining if a string is generated by formal grammar. Unlike many standard evaluations of LLMs' ability to use their context, this task requires composing together a large number of instructions (grammar productions) retrieved from the context. Because the languages are synthetic, the task can be increased in complexity as LLMs' skills improve, and new instances can be automatically generated, mitigating data contamination. We evaluate state-of-the-art LLMs on RELIC and find that their accuracy can be reliably predicted from the complexity of the grammar and the individual example strings, and that even the most advanced LLMs currently available show near-chance performance on more complex grammars and samples, in line with theoretical expectations. We also use RELIC to diagnose how LLMs attempt to solve increasingly difficult reasoning tasks, finding that as the complexity of the language recognition task increases, models switch to relying on shallow heuristics instead of following complex instructions.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces RELIC, a framework for evaluating compositional instruction following in large language models (LLMs) using the task of formal language recognition. RELIC generates context-free grammars (CFGs) of varying complexities and tests whether LLMs can determine if a given string is derivable from a grammar specified in the context window. The authors evaluate state-of-the-art LLMs on a static benchmark, RELIC-500, and find that their accuracy degrades significantly as the complexity of the grammar and the length of the strings increase, often falling to near-chance levels.  Qualitative and quantitative analyses of the models' chain-of-thought tokens reveal that they often resort to shallow heuristics instead of adhering to the complex instructions of the grammar rules, especially as the task becomes more difficult.  The framework is designed to be resistant to data contamination and benchmark saturation due to its generative nature.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core idea of using formal language recognition to evaluate instruction following in LLMs is relatively novel. While previous work has explored formal language capabilities of LLMs, RELIC stands out due to its focus on *compositional* instruction following *in-context*, its generative nature to avoid data contamination, and the control over the complexity of the languages. It directly addresses a challenge faced by static benchmarks - the lack of control over complexity and potential data contamination.

*   **Significance:** RELIC offers a valuable tool for evaluating and diagnosing LLMs' abilities to follow compositional instructions. The finding that even advanced models struggle with relatively simple CFGs highlights a crucial limitation of current LLMs.  The breakdown of models' strategies under increasing task complexity is also significant, providing insights into how they attempt to solve these problems and where they fail. The framework is very valuable as it will give LLM researchers a better diagnostic tool to probe their LLMs in order to avoid unwanted behavior and improve on a task or set of tasks. The results also suggest that current LLMs are not yet exploiting their context window in a sophisticated way, which is counter to a lot of the narrative surrounding LLMs and a great finding. This is one of the main reasons that I rate this paper so highly.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Generative framework:** The generative approach allows for continuous creation of new, unbiased evaluation instances, mitigating data contamination and benchmark saturation issues.
    *   **Controllable complexity:**  The ability to modulate the complexity of the grammars and examples provides a fine-grained method for probing LLMs' capabilities.
    *   **Diagnostic potential:** The framework allows for detailed analysis of models' reasoning processes and identification of specific failure modes, especially through the chain-of-thought analysis.
    *   **Clear experimental setup and results:**  The paper provides a well-defined experimental setup, comprehensive evaluation of several LLMs, and clear presentation of the results.
    *   **Release of RELIC-500 dataset:** The release of the dataset should incentivize researchers to work on improving on it, and improving their models' ability to perform this type of task.
*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Negative sampling method:** The independent and uniform sampling of tokens for negative examples might introduce statistical biases that could be exploited by heuristic-based strategies. It would be stronger if the negative examples were created in a more sophisticated way.
    *   **Limited scope of formal languages:** While CFGs capture key aspects of compositionality, they are still a simplified representation of natural language and programming languages. Expanding RELIC to more expressive formalisms could provide further insights.
    *   **Reliance on chain-of-thought:** The analysis relies on the LLMs' generated explanations which may or may not be a faithful representation of their actual reasoning process.
    *   **Difficulty scaling complexity:** Although the framework allows increasing complexity, the inherent limitations of transformers in recognizing languages in-context might pose a challenge to scaling beyond certain limits. It's not clear that LLMs can solve context-free language recognition, so the study may be hitting that wall.
    *   **Possible lack of clarity:** The prompt is very hard to parse as-is. However, a more specific example of the format might not be of any additional help, and might provide too much help.

*   **Potential Influence:** RELIC has the potential to become a standard benchmark for evaluating compositional instruction following in LLMs. It can drive research towards developing more robust and reliable models that can effectively utilize context and follow complex instructions. The diagnostic capabilities can also guide the development of improved reasoning mechanisms in LLMs.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper presents a novel framework and contributes valuable insights into the limitations of current LLMs regarding compositional instruction following. RELIC addresses a critical need for robust and scalable evaluation methods, and its diagnostic capabilities provide a pathway for improving model reasoning abilities. While there are some limitations, the strengths significantly outweigh the weaknesses. The potential for impact is high as this work has the potential to greatly help develop LLMs.

### Follow-Your-Motion: Video Motion Transfer via Efficient Spatial-Temporal Decoupled Finetuning
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.05207v1)
- **Authors**: Yue Ma, Yulong Liu, Qiyuan Zhu, Ayden Yang, Kunyu Feng, Xinhua Zhang, Zhifeng Li, Sirui Han, Chenyang Qi, Qifeng Chen
- **Abstract**: Recently, breakthroughs in the video diffusion transformer have shown remarkable capabilities in diverse motion generations. As for the motion-transfer task, current methods mainly use two-stage Low-Rank Adaptations (LoRAs) finetuning to obtain better performance. However, existing adaptation-based motion transfer still suffers from motion inconsistency and tuning inefficiency when applied to large video diffusion transformers. Naive two-stage LoRA tuning struggles to maintain motion consistency between generated and input videos due to the inherent spatial-temporal coupling in the 3D attention operator. Additionally, they require time-consuming fine-tuning processes in both stages. To tackle these issues, we propose Follow-Your-Motion, an efficient two-stage video motion transfer framework that finetunes a powerful video diffusion transformer to synthesize complex motion.Specifically, we propose a spatial-temporal decoupled LoRA to decouple the attention architecture for spatial appearance and temporal motion processing. During the second training stage, we design the sparse motion sampling and adaptive RoPE to accelerate the tuning speed. To address the lack of a benchmark for this field, we introduce MotionBench, a comprehensive benchmark comprising diverse motion, including creative camera motion, single object motion, multiple object motion, and complex human motion. We show extensive evaluations on MotionBench to verify the superiority of Follow-Your-Motion.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "Follow-Your-Motion: Video Motion Transfer via Efficient Spatial-Temporal Decoupled Finetuning":

**Summary:**

The paper addresses the problem of video motion transfer, where the goal is to generate a new video with the motion from a reference video. The authors identify two key issues with current methods that use Low-Rank Adaptation (LoRA) finetuning of video diffusion transformers: motion inconsistency and tuning inefficiency. They propose "Follow-Your-Motion," a two-stage framework that tackles these issues by introducing a spatial-temporal decoupled LoRA. This decoupled LoRA separates the attention architecture into spatial appearance and temporal motion processing. The second stage involves sparse motion sampling and adaptive RoPE (Rotary Positional Embedding) to accelerate training and improve motion interpolation. They also introduce MotionBench, a new benchmark for video motion transfer. Experiments demonstrate improvements in motion consistency, tuning speed, and overall performance compared to existing methods.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:**

*   **Strengths:** The core novelty lies in the spatial-temporal decoupled LoRA. While the idea of decoupling spatial and temporal information in video processing isn't entirely new, its specific application within a DiT (Diffusion Transformer) framework using LoRA for motion transfer is innovative. The introduction of adaptive RoPE to better handle sparse sampling in the temporal domain also adds a novel contribution.
*   **Weaknesses:** The idea of two-stage finetuning with spatial and temporal LoRAs has been explored to some extent in previous works, such as MotionDirector. The current method extends and adapts this approach for diffusion transformers. The novelty of sparse motion sampling is also incremental. RoPE is a known quantity; the adaption of it here is sensible but not a breakthrough.

**Significance:**

*   **Strengths:** The paper makes a practical contribution by addressing the inefficiency of finetuning large video diffusion transformers for motion transfer. The proposed techniques significantly improve tuning speed without sacrificing performance. The introduction of MotionBench fills a gap in the field by providing a comprehensive benchmark for evaluating motion transfer methods. The qualitative results are visually compelling, and the quantitative results demonstrate a clear improvement over baselines.
*   **Weaknesses:** The results section lacks rigorous comparison to MotionCrafter, which is the state-of-the-art method for the area. The gains, though numerically solid, could be better motivated with more analysis of failure modes and clear demonstration of more convincing videos. The user study should be larger than N=20. It does not seem that the project will be released to the public. The paper builds upon existing ideas; it doesn't represent a fundamental shift in the field.

**Justification for the Score:**

The paper offers a solid engineering contribution to video motion transfer. The spatial-temporal decoupled LoRA, combined with sparse sampling and adaptive RoPE, presents a practical and effective solution for improving tuning efficiency and motion consistency in DiT-based models. The introduction of MotionBench provides a valuable resource for the research community. While the individual components may not be groundbreaking, their combination and application within the context of video diffusion transformers are novel and lead to significant performance gains. However, the paper lacks certain crucial state-of-the-art baselines and has a small-sized user study.
